About The Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is
about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level
LAND AREA:
26.63 Square Miles
TOTAL AREA:
29 Square Miles
TOWN ROADS:
77.51 Miles
STATE ROADS:
6 Miles
POPULATION (1975 State Census):
8,175
DENSITY:
306 Persons Per Square Mile
ASSESSED VALUATION:
$67,926,470.00 (Real Estate)
$ 3,313,000.00 (Personal Property)
TAX RATE:
$49.65

===PAGE 6===
Board of Selectmen
ANN P. HALL
SHERWOOD G WINCH:
RUDOLPH MASELLA
Board of Selectmen: left to right - Ann P. Hall, Chairman Sherwood G. Winchester and Rudolph Masella.
The Board of Selectmen, also serving as Board of Health and Licensing Authority, never have a dull agenda.
State and Federal changes in laws which regulate the operation of a municipality are never-ending. Budget season
is particularly time-consuming. With growth and new programs, inflated cost of operation, Selectmen are always
trying to find a balance between sensible spending, and providing the necessary services we need to live with. The
goal to achieve is "the best possible services at the lowest possible cost".
Proposition 13 -- its success in California has brought an awareness to the voter that something can be done
about their taxes. More importantly, it has made government aware that the voter will be heard and that things
will happen to not only stop increases but also reduce their tax burden. Hopefully, here in Massachusetts our Gov-
ernor and the Legislature will take heedof this possibility and reduce the number of programs which mandate to cities
and towns costly programs for which no State support monies are made available, thus causing local property taxes to
rise. The mood appears to be there in Boston -- let US all hope that the mood changes into action.
The problem of solid waste is still with us, and just like the daily garbage, it won't go away by itself. The
present landfill on Townsend Harbor Road is rapidly nearing its approved capacity, and the Board of Health will most
probably be forced to close the facility sometime in the coming year. A majority of the voters present at the Special
Town Meeting on June 12, 1978 approved the recommendations of the Refuse Disposal Planning Committee and the
Board of Selectmen, but the vote fell short of the required two-thirds, thus delaying a Town decision on future solid
waste disposal. The Board fervently hopes the voters of Lunenburg will make a decision to resolve the problem of
waste disposal in a manner acceptable to Town residents and to the State alike before further State and/or judicial
action makes the decision for US.
Lunenburg does notrun on elected officials alone. About one hundred civic-minded residents serve on various
committees, shaping and molding ways and means to run our Town. The Selectmen wish to thank them for their time
and supporting efforts.
- 4 -
===PAGE 7===
Lunenburg residents were treated to a once in alifetime year in 1978. On January 1st there was aflag raising
on the Common which was to signal a year-long celebration of our 250th Anniversary. The 250th Anniversary Com-
mittee spent five years of planning and hard work to bring back the memories of 250 years. Among the many high-
lights of the year was an outstanding pageant, written and produced by Lunenburg residents, and enjoyed by all.
In October, Lunenburg was treated to the best parade we have ever had in our Town. Yes, Lovable Lunenburg is
very much alive and has come a long way since 1728. Thanks to all who participated in this marvelous year.
Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors: left to right - William F. Reed, Chairman John F. Nicoll and Bernard J. Paton.
The Board of Assessors has continued to attempt to maintain equality in their assessments by reviewing all prop-
erties, maintaining and evaluating sales records, and on-site viewing. The State Department of Revenue continues
to demand "full and fair market value" on all property. Lunenburg has factored several increases, however, these
increases are not keeping up with the inflation rate.
Although building permits are required for all new buildings and for alterations, the Board still finds many vio-
lations. New construction continues at a low rate with 26 new home starts last year. However, there has been a
considerable amount of alterations and out buildings added.
The Board has attended many meetings and seminars sponsored by the State and/or Assessor's Association to keep
informed of new laws and changes in the present ones. The new maps which were votedin at the last Town Meeting
were made and delivered. Included were also two small books which allows the Board to carry them in the field for
those areas where it is difficult to identify properties.
- 5 -
===PAGE 8===
We wish to comment on the passage of Chapter 580, which was also known as " Proposition "on the ballot in
the November 1978 election and how the new law effects the assessment program in Lunenburg. We are under a man-
date from the Bureau of Revenue to bring the assessed valuation of the Town to 100% full and fair market value. We
have filed and had accepted, a plan of action to bring the Town to 100% over a period of years, and we have made
two 10% increases in all real properties in 1976 and 1977 to gradually comply with the mandate. We took no addi-
tional action in 1978 due to a severe decrease in new construction coupled with a considerable number of bank sales
of property which the banks had acquired through bankruptcies or foreclosures. We decided to wait and see if the
economy was cooling off prior to completing our 100% assessment program. We are now awaiting the completion of
guidelines from the State for implementation of the new law.
We, in the interval, remain under the mandate of the court to complete our re-valuation and arrive at one
hundred per cent valuation of all property in the Town. The new law also requires one hundred per cent valuation
and an inspection and certification by the State prior to setting the proportional percentages (55% Industrial, 50%
Commercial, and 45% residential with a $5000 valuation automatic abatement) on which the tax rate will be calcu-
lated. A house with a 100% value of $40,000 will be assessed at 45% or $18,000 less $5,000 abatement or $13,000.
The tax rate will be set by dividing the total taxable value of the Town into the amount of monies voted at Town
Meeting, and the result will be the rate per thousand. Tax bills will show both 100% valuation and taxable valua-
tion on each parcel of property.
We have been informed that the State Legislature will be asked to define "open land" which under the new law
will be valued at 100% and taxed on 25% of that valuation. We may find that the State will in effect be setting the
value of land throughout the Commonwealth. We feel that with several court cases already questioning the consti-
tutionality of the new law, coupled with the lack of guidelines and the questions on open land, the Town would be
better served by adopting a "wait and see" policy this year.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concerning the
value of residential property, owned and occupied as a domicile by persons in various groups for whom the payment
of taxes could constitute an undue burden. These exemptions may not exceed specific amounts. If a taxpayer feels
that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the Assessors' Office and request an appli-
cation which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section
17 - Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation or a tax of
$175, whichever is greater.
Section
22 - Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 valuation or
a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E - Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100% disability.
Section
37
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section
41
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $500, whichever is greater. Some of these
amounts are in the process of being changed by the State Legislature.
Section 41A - Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over with gross receipts of under $20,000 annually
to place the taxes on his domicile andone acre of land on a deferred payment plan, the taxes to be
paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section
42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation or
a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43
- Minor orphans of police officersor firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation or a tax
of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61A
Section 9
- Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agricultural or
horticultural use.

===PAGE 10===
Library Trustees
The year 1978 witnessed the completion of the reorganizationof the Library's facilities begun last year. Steel
bookshelves, purchased second-hand from another library, now hold the nonfiction collection in the first two rooms,
upstairs. The back room upstairs, formerly the nonfiction room, has been
converted into a useful study area/meeting room. This room was carpeted,
hung with plants, and repainted with funds raised by the Friends of the
Ritter Memorial Library.
The twelve month reorganization has substantially improved the
Library's services and accommodations, yet really cost the taxpayersonly
a minimum amount of money. Careful purchasing of materials, shopping
for top quality used furniture, and performing all the labor ourselves paid
off by obtaining $8, 109.00 worth of merchandise for $2,618.00. Funds
from Library Trust Funds and the Friends of the Library further offset the
cost to the Town to a mere $385.00 -- less than 5% of the list cost.
The Library, as a result of this reorganizing effort, now has a
separate roomfor children's programs and collections, an integrated non-
fiction section, an attractive meeting room available for use by community
groups, and a main floor with the most requested and necessary materials
available to all. The primary focus has been to make it easier for all
people to use the Library's resources,
The period between dismant ling old shelves
and installing new ones required arranging
books anywhere there wasavailable space.
During 1978 the Library took advantage of these
improvements to offer more programs and services than
ever before. To celebrate Lunenburg's 250th Anniver-
sary, the Library sponsored several slide shows and lec-
tures on the Town's history, graciously delivered by
Margaret P. Harley. Another very popular historical
program was this summer's "Adventures in Time" series
for children, which included a slide show, a tour of the
Historical Society, and also a trip through the Cushing
House, the Town's oldest surviving house. The Friends
of the Library arranged for the beautiful celebration,
Balloon Day, held in May on the school grounds. They
also designed and constructed a beautiful float for the
Anniversary Day Parade in October. These events were
tributes from the Library to Lunenburg and its heritage.
Diverse activities are crucial to children's services
in today's libraries and the Ritter Memorial Library pro-
vided many opportunities tailored to the Town's younger
members. The summer program offered movies, a sing-
a-long, a puppet show, group participation stories by
the Little Red Wagon group, and a talk and demonstra-
tion on seeing-eye dogsby a blind man, Philip Oliver.
Balloon Day, coordinated by the Friends of the Library and
This year also featured a puppet-making workshop, cro-
the Elementary School, was a sight to behold. Postcards
cheting classes, Christmas ornament projects, the chil-
attached to the balloons were mailed back from as far away
dren's art festival, and the ever-popular preschool story
as Long Island's south shore!
hours. Librarian John Ramsay conducted classes on deaf
- 8 -
===PAGE 11===
sign language for Middle School age students. A particularly successful event was a Halloween-time showing of the
original King Kong movie, viewed at the Elementary School by some 300 screaming children! Young people surely
had much to get excited about at the Library in 1978.
Special programs were also very much in evidence for the rest of Lunenburg's population this year. The Friends'
Interest Series ranged from knitting wool sweaters, resuming careers, and herbal remedies to massage and counseling.
There were two "International Nights", with slides, discussions and foods fromdifferent foreign countries of interest
to our Townspeople. Sonja Foss presented a view of her native Holland and Ruth Hoag and Ted Agnault covered
Scandinavia for very appreciative audiences. Films once again were shown on Sunday nights. Some of them were
Rules of the Game, Brian's Song, Suspicion, David and Lisa, The African Queen and Room at the Top.
Circulation figures show both the effectsof inflation on the book-buying power of the budget and the conversion
of the Annex to a Primary School for the kindergarten and first grades. Circulation of adult books kept pace with
last year's figures, with a substantial increase in the use of paperback books. Record borrowing is down, perhaps
because there has been no provision for audio-visual materials in the Library budget for the past few years. Year-
end totals for adult items were: 18, 957 books (75 interlibrary loans), 873 records, 1, 200 magazines and 44 pamphlets;
66 films were borrowed through the regional library system.
A new pattern is emerging in the circulation of children's books. The summer reading program placed a definite
emphasis onolder children, and as a result the summer circulation figures were down from their all-time high in 1977.
Another change resulted from the move of the third graders from the Annex to the Elementary School. For several
years these students were the most enthusiastic users of all children's materials, and in their weekly visits to the Li-
brary they learned to make effective use of Library resources. Now that these children moved from the Annex to the
Elementary School and no longer come to the Library on a regular basis, circulation by class visits in nonfiction and
fiction for older readers have both declined by 99%. The Library limits the number of borrowed by the kinder-
garten children and first graders now visiting from the former Annex, since these students are just learning how to use
the Library, and, therefore, overall class circulation is down by about 50%. Children's circulation figures for 1978
were: 22,079 books, 143 magazines, 33 puzzles, 136 filmstrip kits and 549 records.
The constraints imposed by runaway inflation in book prices over the past several years is being keenly felt at
the Ritter Memorial Library. Fewer books can be purchased with budgeted funds each year. Fortunately, the gener-
osity of several individuals and community groups has provided resources over and above the regular Library budget.
A fine set of books has been given by Mrs. Sandra Demers to honor the 50th Wedding Anniversary of her parents, Mr.
& Mrs. Leo Molter; Mr. Edward Balcom has given outstanding reference volumes in memory of his wife Patricia Balcom
and son Edward. Mrs. Kathryn Curtis has remembered her father, the Library's original architect, Frederick L. Francis,
with a display case for recent fiction. The Lunenburg Women's Club presented the Library with money for a book in
memory of Georgia Brown, and the Lunenburg Memorial Fund remembered David Ellms with shelves and a display stand
for the Memorial Fund record book, and provided a collection of books in memory of Harold Welterlen.
Generosity is also the hallmark of a group whose endeavors have influenced virtually every phase of the Library's
services, resources and furnishings. The Friends of the Ritter Memorial Library have raised funds to purchase a card
catalog, atlas case, carpeting, and wood for shelves as part of the Library reorganization. Their undertakings have
included yard sales, clothing sales, an Open House, co-sponsored with the Library Trustees, nature walks, the interest
series, children's art festival, boutique table, and several decoration and craft workshops. Also, members of the
Friends frequently volunteer whenever extra people are needed to help the Library staff carry out programs. This
group has been invaluable to the Library and, therefore, the Town. Anyone who feels that the Ritter Memorial Li-
brary is an important part of Lunenburg's charm and value cannot do better than to join the Friends and participate
in their activities.
In sum, new programs, changing circulation patterns, a highly successful reorganization of facilities, and the
generosity of many individuals and groups have all played key roles in making 1978 one of the most event-full and
active years in the history of the Ritter Memorial Library.
- 9
===PAGE 12===
Park Commission
The 1978 Summer Program was a huge success under the leadership of our Director, Richard Stachowicz. He
was ably assisted by Karen Lindsay at the Wallis Park Playground and Cindy St. Arnaud at the Center Playground.
Both of these playgrounds were well attended because of the quality and enthusiasm of both instructors. We should
also not fail to mention the fine Arts & Crafts Program under the direction of Gina DiGiammo and Joan Carey.
Men's Softball had a very good year with Web-nik Club winning both the League Championship and the Play-
off Championship. The only drawback was that we had to refuse entry to some teams because of lack of available
space. We do hope to expand our softball program to include more teams. We have in the works the possibility of
a softball field on school-owned land off West Street. Plans are being formulated between the School Committee
and the Park Commission for use of this land. This property would not only be used for a softball field but also pos-
sibly for one or two tennis courts as well.
Summer High School Basketball ran very smoothly. Lunenburg won its first League Championship ever by
beating Fitchburg. Junior League Street Hockey and Basketball had a super year under the direction of David Foster.
Also, the Summer Recreation Program concluded another year with its thirteenth Annual Muscular Dystrophy fund-
raiser. A total of $274.80 was netted for this most worthy cause.
The Town Beach was ably directed by Gerry Beloin. Fortunately, there were no serious accidents. The life-
guards are required by the Park Commission to be trained in CPR. We are very much impressed with the swim in-
struction and life-saving programs conducted at the Town Beach.
Jack Therrien instructed the Tennis Program in our Town. He instructed 127 people last summer. This figure
can be broken down as follows: 9-13 years of age 56; Whalom (youth and adult) - 25; Teens - 6; Adults - 40.
We have been aware of the condition of the tennis courts for a number of years. This year weplan to propose at the
Town Meeting an Article requesting slightly over $5,000 for lining and filling of all existing tennis courts both at
Wallis Park and at Marshall Park. This has not been done for a number of years and is badly needed.
We were able to place in our budget for 1979-1980 the sum of $1,000 for Equipment Replacement and Repair.
We, as Commissioners, along with our Summer Recreation Director, Richard Stachowicz, feel that some of our play-
ground equipment is and needs to be replaced. We want your children to have an enjoyable and safe experience
in our playgrounds. We are pleased that the Finance Committee approved $1,000 to purchase playground equipment.
Chuck Wennerberg's Girls' Softball Team won for a second year in a row. The team which was made up of
fifteen girls was ably coached by Donna Moilanen. The Women's Physical Fitness Program under the direction of
Rick Hens went better than expected. Teenage Basketball was conducted on Sunday afternoons by Ronald Lamothe.
There were four teams with thirty participants.
The BOR Report is a five-year fund of Open Space Recreation and Conservation Plan. The Conservation and
Recreation Commission's membership is made up of the following individuals: Richard J. Bishop, Nancy 1. Sholl,
Harold D. Harley, Richard A. Brockelman, Mary L. Padula, Armand E. St. Arnaud and Patrick J. Slattery. This
Commission completed its five-year action plan for the Town of Lunenburg and has submitted the plan to Boston.
It is the hope of this Commission that in the very near future Lunenburg will receive some federal and state funding.
The state and federal governments will pay up to 50% of the total cost of the project.
The Park Commission wishes to thank all who in any way helped to make the various programs so successful.
Thanks to Karen Reed for helping out in our office (Tuesdays 3-5PM and Saturdays 9-11AM). Special thanks to Town
Clerk, Mary Padula, and her excellent staff. A very special thank you to our Supervisor, Bob Garneau and his able
assistant, Greg Bingham, who kept our facilities in excellent condition. We have heard comments about the good
job being done in fixing up the fields.
In closing, the Commissioners would like to appeal to the Townspeople to support US at the Annual Town Meet-
ing. We feel that we have your interests at heart why not have ours at heart.

Cemetery Commission
The Cemetery Commissioners met with the Cemetery Superintendent regularly throughout the year to insure op-
erational effectiveness. No major itemsof equipment were purchased, and repair and maintenance of current equip-
ment was effected as required with the exception of the lowering device which required extensive repairs and re-
fitting. The company effecting repair has provided a lowering device for use until repairs are completed.
Grounds maintenance of both cemeteries was efficiently performed, and plans are approved for the replace-
ment of certain damaged road hedges, and for the removal of diseasedor damaged trees to enhance the overall grounds
appearance.

===PAGE 14===
Housing Authority
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its ninth year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street known
as the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Presently, we have forty-two apartments occupied by single persons and
six apartments occupied by couples,
The tenants have their own organized Pearl Brook Tenants' Association, which meets on a regular basis and
arranges interesting programs and projects for the benefit and enjoyment of its members. Mrs. Helen Byron holds the
office of President. She is the principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members.
The requirements for occupancy are that applicants be at least 65 years of age and in need of good housing.
Rentsare variable in that no more than 25% of income can be charged for rent which includes heat, water and elec-
tricity. We invite anyone who is interested to inquire at the Housing Authority Office where Mrs. Eileen Alleva,
Executive Secretary, will be available to answer questions.
The tenants of the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly participate in and enjoy the many interesting activities
held throughout the year and also take an active part in community affairs.
Housing Authority: Seated left to right - Executive Secretary Eileen M. Alleva,
Ella Rae Denault and Josephine C. Meloon. Standing left to right - Richard 1.
White, Chairman Reverend Richard W. Dupee and George E. Webster, Jr.

===PAGE 15===
Council on Aging
The year 1978 proved to be another eventful year of progress for the Council on Aging. The Council initiated
a pilot transportation program, enabling the senior citizens to be provided with this service two days a week. The
program, which began in February, is designed for medical, dental, banking, shopping and personal needs. Partici-
pation has increased each month, and the Council has now established the program on a permanent basis.
The "Meals on Wheels" program is now in its third year, serving the elderly shut-ins. The continuing success
of this venture can only be attributed to all the volunteers who have given so unselfishly of their time. Many thanks
to all who have assisted throughout the year.
It was a real pleasure for Council members to participate in Lunenburg's 250th Birthday Parade in October. The
Sixth Annual Christmas Dinner was held at the High School, with a superb meal prepared by Mrs. Marjorie Stillman
and her personnel, served by an energetic group of students and enjoyed by all present, including many of our own
Council members. Donald Adams and his choral group provided a very pleasurable musical program following dinner.
Several new members were appointed to the Council during the past year, and we welcome Reverend John E.
Sandberg of the Pilgrim Covenant Church and Richard F. Beardmore, Jr. The Council deeply regrets the loss of our
Council member Eino A. Liloiva, who passed away in March, and also the resignation of Reverend Richard W. . Dupee
who had served the Council so faithfully since its origin.
Councilon Aging: Seated left to right - Lucienne Chapdelaine, Chairman Martha L.
Molaghan, Elvira Z. May and Marcella W. Peed. Standing left to right - Milton C.
Carpenter, Alfred C. Gilchrest, Richard F. Beardmore, Jr., Reverend John E.
Sandberg and Mark G. Thatcher, Sr. Members not present when picture was taken
were Arthur J. Brockelman, Doris M. Carrier and Barbara F. Jordan.

===PAGE 16===
Personnel Board
Personnel Board: left to right- - Frank H. DeBettencourt, Chairman Jacqueline A. Keeler and Michael J.
Freel. Members not present when picture was taken were Robert P. McNally and John L. Plank, Jr.
It is a truism to say that local Personnel officials must be ever on the alert to enactments by the State Legis-
lature because their personnel programs may be affected by these State laws. In 1977 there were over 1000 laws en-
acted as the State Legislature met in a year-long session.
Surrounded by an inflationary economy, the Personnel Board recognizes it must adapt and adjust the Salary
Administration Plan to meet the needs of the Town employees as these rapidly changing conditions appear.
Each year, the Board meets with the Commissions and Department Heads, who care to do so, to review the ex-
isting Salary Administration Plan and to consider appropriate changes.
The many factors which affect the Plan have been carefully considered and, where appropriate, included in a
revised Plan which respectfully will be presented to the voters of Lunenburg for their consideration. The Board thinks
the revised Plan represents a balanced and fair proposal for the Town employees and the taxpayers, and recommends
it be adopted by the Town.
The following is an excerpt from the Salary Administration Plan of the Town of Lunenburg:
"The Personnel Committee is hereby authorized to ad-
minister the provisions of thisby-law, except for such duties
as may be specifically assigned by statute or by-law to other
town officers, boards or committees. Appeals from decisions
of the Personnel Committee may be taken to the Town at the
next Annual or Special Town Meeting."

Building Inspector
The Tri-Town Building Inspector wishes at this time
to take the opportunity to thank Lunenburg residents and
Town fficials for their cooperation and assistance through-
out the year.
The Building Inspector's Office is in the Legion House
at 27 Memorial Drive (Telephone: 582-4613) and the of-
fice hours are 8:00 A.M. to 4:00 P. M. Monday thru Friday.
Applications for building permits may be obtained at, and
completed applications delivered to, the Selectmen's Of-
fice at the Town Hall.
The Building Inspector would like to again this year
remind residents of the Town that a permit is required to in-
stall a wood-burning stove. This requirement is for your
own protection, as there have been far too many house fires
caused by faulty installation of wood stoves.
As of January 1, 1978, the State Building Code has
adopted Article 22 on Energy Conservation as follows:
Austin R. Fitzsimmons, Building Inspector
"SECTION 2200.0 SCOPE
This article sets forth requirements for buildings
and structures or portions thereof, additions, and alter-
ations, hereafter designed primarily for human occupancy,
covering their exterior envelopes and selection of their
heating, ventilating and air conditioning, service water
heating, electrical distribution, and illuminating systems
and equipment for effective use of energy."

Highway Department
We can be grateful that finally Congress re-
cently saw fit to pass: a meaningful Highway Bill
and extend the trust fund for another four years.
The $51 billion, although short of needs and sharing
with mass transit, represents amajor accomplishment
and a tribute to its proponents in the Senate and
the House. Highways are paid for in advance by
the people who use them -- for the benefit of all.
The people have every right to expect they'l get
what they're paying for, including the proper fund-
ing to maintain the more than 3.8 billion miles of
roads, streets and bridges.
Passing the Bill, however, was just part of
the battle. In this instance, if past is prologue,
we hope the adage does not prevail. With the un-
veiling of the administration's planto slow the ec-
onomy to fight inflation, the thought of impound-
ment raises its ugly potential action that has de-
layed projects and increased highway costsby bil-
lions in the past twenty years. Should that happen
to the present allocations in the new Bill, it could
lead to disaster and would be a totally irrational
move in light of the fact that the funds have been
paid in advance and are needed now.
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
Whichbrings the great pot-hole mystery.
You may recall the $200 million urgently allocated this spring for emergency use on the 120 million pot holes resulting
from delays in needed maintenance even before the severe winters of 1977 and 1978 brought this about. It appears
that part of the Bill was given a last-minute change before passing. The change calls for "Water Projects". What
that has to do with pot holes is difficult to understand. It obviously takes more than water to fill them. (Editorial
Viewpoint: Better Roads Magazine)
The following is a report of services performed and projects accomplishedon the Town's approximately 80 miles
of accepted roads:
Chapter 90 Improvement - Mix-in-place surfaces were appliedon Lancaster Avenue, Shirley Road and Electric Ave-
nue. Drainage was installed in Leominster Road and Electric Avenue and guard rail purchased at a cost of $43,638.02
including a balance from last year.
General Highway Maintenance - Approximately 557.62 tons of patch was used on roads all over Town at a cost of
$15,711.78 for labor and materials. Catch basins were cleaned out and drainage work performed on various roads.
Brush cutting, grading, roadside mowing, gravelling, sweeping, purchase and installation of guard rail, purchase of
small tools cost $74,416.80 of which $31,750.65 was spent for 51,012 gallons of asphalt and labor under the Road
Oil & Tar section of this account.
Snow Removal - Due to the blizzard of 1978, our winter operations cost $106,515.09. However, the U. S. Army
Corp of Engineers paid the hired equipment contractors $5,925.50. The Town of Lunenburg was reimbursed $3,433
by HUD/FDAA. We are very grateful for the financial assistance rendered by these two organizations.
Road Machinery Maintenance - This account includes the maintenance and repair of all the Highway Department's
equipment, purchase of parts, plow and grader blades, chains, gas, oil, grease, diesel fuel, acetylene, oxygen and
small tools. The operation of this account cost $41,846.95, including a small balance from last year.

Town's approximately 80 miles of accepted roads. A total of 13,681 gallons of asphalt was purchased and applied.
Guard rail, pipe, catch basins, gravel and patch were purchased at a total cost of $50,725.51.

Town Forest Committee
In 1923 a Committee was set up to
investigate the feasibility of a town for-
est. It was decided to purchase 125 acres
off Gilchrest Street for this purpose. Some
twenty-six years later in 1949, the Sel-
ectmen appointed three members to the
first Town Forest Committee. They were:
Irving Kimball, Carl Brown, Sr. and Law-
rence Bigelow.
The Lunenburg Conservation Com-
mission was appointed in 1961 and for the
past eighteen years these two groups held
joint meetings and carriedout a joint pro-
gram rated by many asoneof the most suc-
cessful in the entire state. These two
groups now carry out separate programs.
The present Committee is Harold D.
Harley, Chairman; Gwendolyn K. Holmes,
Secretary and Armas S.J. Heino, Treas-
urer. There aresix Town Forest areas with
a total of 194 acres under their supervi-
Town Forest Committee: left to right - Armas S. J. Heino,
sion.
Gwendolyn K. Holmes and Chairman Harold D. Harley.

Police Department
The enactment of Criminal Law Training re-
quirements mandating full-time officers to satisfac-
torily complete an approved twelve week training
school or part-time officers to pass a written exam-
ination before exercising police powers, in addition
to already existing laws requiring basic first aid and
CPR training, puts the Lunenburg Police Department
on a professional level comparable with any in the
Commonwealth. To date, seven part-time officers
have passed the written examination, To help pre-
pare the part-time officers for these examinations,
training courses are being held locally with Attorney
Timothy Hillman as Instructor.
Staff changes during 1978 included: In March,
the resignation of Patrolman Michael A. Bottalico
whose resignation was accepted with regret; Patrol-
man John E. Baker was named as his replacement;
In July, David S. Delisle was added to the Depart-
ment bringing the complement to a total of nine.
Both Patrolmen Delisle and Barney completed a 12
week basic training program, and Patrolman Baker
is scheduled to undergo similar training in January.
"Job Stress" has become a critical subject in
Frederick B. Hobbs, Chief of Police
police work, and this issue has been examined with
representatives of the Mental Health Center in Fitch-
burg. Support sessions were conducted for Department members as an in-service program. The new "Family Violence
Law", along with court reorganization procedures, has also necessitated staff preparedness.
In 1978, 6, 363 calls for service were received by the Police Department. These included five armed robberies,
119 breaks into private homes and businesses, 146 larcenies of personal property and 33 stolen motor vehicles. The
dollar value of all property stolen in Lunenburg amounted to $113,088, and the recovery of stolen property equalled
$37,984 or a 34% recovery record.
It appeared until late in November of 1978 that Lunenburg might go through a "fatal free" accident year
when Route 2A again denied US this distinction by taking three lives in fourteen days. It is interesting and tragic to
note that since 1974, Lunenburg has had eleven fatal accidents, taking twelve lives. Ten of these accidents OC-
curred on Route 2A. Nine accidents taking lives were in the area which was under consideration for reconstruc-
tion. An additional 337 accidents were investigated including 44 hit and run (both property damage and personal
injury). Also, 158 acts of vandalism and 45 assaults, five of which were on police officers, were investigated.
There were 891 house checks made, 226 summons served, 281 gun permits issued, 126 people taken into protective
custody and 468 alarms (false or otherwise) responded to, As a resultof stopping 1,691 motor vehicles, 646 citations
were issued. Some other activities requiring police investigation or assistance were disturbances, sudden deaths or
suicides, trespassing, annoying or obscene telephone calls, drug complaints, child abuse and neglect, and a variety
of other complaints handled as part of routine police duties.
Lunenburg's celebration of our 250th Anniversary during 1978 will be long remembered by all. It is gratifying
to know that no policing expenses were incurred for any of the many activities directly sponsored by the 250th An-
niversary Committee. Personal thanks are extended to the following officers: George E. Aho, John M. Alward,
John E. Baker, Stanley W. Barney, Alphonse J. Baron, James A. Bertram, Richard J. Constantine, Scott W. Curtis,
William J. Deming, Jr., Robert H. Demont, Guilford M. Dunn III, George A. Funnell, Denise R. Kindschi, Rich-
ard L. Lambert, Jr., Douglas R. J. Latini, Donald A. Letarte, Bruce W. Martin, Richard R. Moeckel, Hector J.

Fire Department
In 1978 the Fire Department answered 444 calls for
assistance as follows: Rescue Unit Calls 256; Service
Calls 44; Mutual Aid 37; Brush Fires 25; Car Fires 18;
Chimney Fires 17; Stand By at Accidents 11; Needless
Calls 9; Building Fires 9; Electrical Fires 5; Alarms 3;
Dryer Fires 2; Water Rescue 2; Food on Stove 1; Town
Dump 1; Hay Wagon 1; Lawn Mower 1; Sign Fire I; Oil
Burner 1. The number of fire calls decreased by 19%
below the 1977 figure. The categories with the largest
decreases were Service Calls, Mutual Aid Calls, Brush
Fires and Needless Calls, The Rescue Unit Calls showed
an increase of 23% and the number of these calls will
probably continue to increase to about the 300 mark be-
fore it levels off. The Fire Chief believes that Lunen-
burg's population will have a direct bearingon this figure.
The four fire trucks and one rescue/ambulance are
in good condition and no major repairs or replacements
are anticipated for at least two years at which time con-
sideration should be given to rebuilding or replacing the
1961 pumper.
During the year training sessions were held for Emer-
gency Medical Technicians and for firefighters. All fire- -
fighters have been trained as First Responders (CPR and
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
First Aid) and nine Emergency Medical Technicians were
recertified for ambulance work which is required every
two years. Eight firefighters attended fire training school in Fitzwilliam, New Hampshire.
The Men's Muster Team won first place in the Massachusetts Muster League competing with twenty other Fire
Departments. The Women's Muster Team came in third in their division.
The Fire Chief would like to thank the officers and members of the Fire Department for their cooperation and
assistance throughout the year.
ROSTER OF FIREMEN FOLLOWS: (*Denotes service on Rescue Unit also.)
*James P. Deming, Chief
*Henry D. Kelley, Jr., Assistant Chief
Ralph M. Bickford, Jr., 1st Lieutenant

Dog Officer
There were approximately 1150 dogs licensed
in Lunenburg in 1978, due in part to sending out
notices and twenty hours of telephone calls.
This Office received and investigated about
1200 to 1500 calls during 1978. We were forced
to ask that permanent restraining orders be placed
on ten dogs due to their vicious dispositions,
There is still a big problem with dogs whose
owners live in close proximity to the schools. These
dogs follow the children to school and cause a dan-
ger there. Please care for our children, and keep
these dogs at home.
There is a continuing problem with dogown-
ers who work all day and leave their dogs to run
loose and unattended in their absence. These dogs
can sometimes harrass residents living in the neigh-
borhood. If you care for your dogs -- know where
they are.
Dog owners are again reminded that their
dogs should always wear a suitable collar and li-
cense tag and should be kept under close family
supervision. The collar and license tag should be
checked weekly by a family member and should
the license tag ever become lost, a replacement
may be purchased in the Town Clerk's Office for
ten cents. This will help to avoid the many in-
Audrey J. Misner, Dog Officer
cidents of lost and stray dogs.
Refuse Disposal Planning Committee
The Refuse Disposal Planning Committee consists of Chairman Ralph M. Bickford, Jr., Einard M. Warpula
and Robert C. Leupold. The Committee meets the first Thursday of each month in the Town Hall.
The Refuse Disposal Planning Committee has been actively involved in evaluating future refuse disposal alter-
natives due to the impending closure of the existing disposal area. A new landfill, transfer to a private disposal
area, and resource recovery alternatives have been investigated in conjunction with the Board of Health, State
engineers and consulting engineers. The landfill alternative is presently the most economical for the Town. Geo-
logical and watershed maps have been used by the Committee to determine suitable landfill sites.
The Committee has discussed landfill design and planning with consulting engineers, Recommendations for
future refuse disposal will be presented by the Committee at the 1979 Annual Town Meeting.
- 21
===PAGE 24===
Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee
In the first year of its existence the Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee determined that there are
sufficient sewerage and/or drainage problems existing in certain areas of the Town to warrant continued study in a
search for possible solutions to the problem. The Committee has also determined that both Federal and State agencies
have made recent changes in their policies concerning funding of sewerage facilities.
In the past, the official positionof the State and Federal agencieswas that the only solution to sewerage prob-
lems was extensive sewer systems leading to elaborate sewerage treatment plants providing for full and complete
treatment of sewerage. Recently, however, the agencies have changed their position and will now consider alterna-
tive methods of collection and treatment and, in fact, require that any proposed grant requests clearly demonstrate
that alternatives have been considered and that the recommended project is the most cost-effective solution to the
particular problem at hand.
With these thoughts in mind, the Committee retained the engineering firm of Dufresne-Henry. The engineers
were asked to investigate financial assistance programs currently available from State and Federal agencies for al-
ternative solutions for collection and treatment of sewerage which would be less extensive and less expensive than
the previously submitted sewer plan. There are, indeed, funds available for studies and construction of alternative
systems.
One major point which we wish to emphasize is that, as a condition of any of the Federal grants for study
and/or construction of sewerage facilities in the Town, a very basic requirement is that the entire Town be studied.
This does not imply that sewerage must be installed in the entire Town, but it does require that the initial investi-
gation study the entire Town and determine those areas requiring attention and the extent of any problems elsewhere
in the Town.
Representatives of Dufresne-Henry and this Committee met with State and Federal agencies for the purposes
of determining the feasibility of facilities planning to study effective alternative systems, the possibility of recouping
a portion of the Town funds already expended for engineering and design services related to the previously planned
connection to the Fitchburg sewers and to obtain possible implementation schedule for facilities planning and ob-
taining a priority rating for the Town for the above funds.
As a result of these meetings, and after a careful consideration of the reports submitted by Dufresne-Henry
to this Committee, we feel that there are alternatives for solving the most acute problems in the Town and particularly
in the Whalom Area at a cost considerably below the previous amounts that would have been necessary for a complete
sewer system connected to the Fitchburg Treatment Plant.
It is the recommendation of the Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee that the 1979 Annual Town
Meeting appropriate the sum of $18,000.00 to be matched by a Federal EPA Grant of $54,000.00, such sums to be
used to engage an engineering firm to make a detailed study to update the data from the 1973 Morgenroth report,
to identify existing problem areas, to determine future needs, to identify alternative plans to meet existing and also
future needs, and to prepare a preliminary plan for all reasonable alternatives and perform such surveys and make
such reports as required to support the evaluation and the development of alternative plans and reports as may be
required to meet the Federal and State Grant requirements.
We wish to emphasize that the above sum of money is to be used for engineering studies only and, at this time,
we are not recommending any appropriation for construction of any specific facilities at any specific location in the
community. The purpose of this study is to determine the particular extent of the problems in this community and
what would be the most cost-effective method of solving these problems. It is our opinion that this is the most pru-
dent course for the Town to follow at this time.
The Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee is made up of the following members: Chairman Bernard M.
Freeman, David D. Blake, William J. Kearney, Mary L. Padula and Chester J. Pliska,

Tree Warden
One hundred twenty-eight diseased trees were
cut, chipped and buried. The Department of Natural
Resources gave five days ($600.00) of tree removal work
and two daysof tagging trees suspected of having Dutch
Elm disease. These samples were sent to the University
of Massachusetts for analysis. The Town was reimbursed
$521. by the County. A total of $8, 830.00 was spent
under the Dutch Elm account.
Work under the supervision of the Tree Warden
amounted to $6, 110.83. The Tree Warden wishes to re-
mind residents that most pesticides are poisonous. Spray
only when pest control is essential. Read and follow all
directions carefully.
The Tree Warden appreciates the cooperation and
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden
assistance he received throughout the year.
Veterans' Services
In compliance with the request that costs be kept down
during Fiscal Year 1980, this Department will not seek any
increases in payroll, expenses or other budget appropria-
tions. However, should Town employees be given an in-
crease, this Department would then wish to be considered
for the same increase.
Your Veterans' Agent attempted to keep costs down,
but found this very difficult due in part to the spiralling
costs of nursing home service (which received a 3.2% in-
crease in per diem rates) and also due to increased costs in
other categories such as Medication, Dental, Hospitaliza-
tion, etc. Our caseload at present is quite low, and hope-
fully it will remain so. However, this Department can, at
any time, be confronted with extremely high billsor state-
ments from veterans who areor have been hospitalized and
have no other means with which to pay for the services ren-
dered. There is now a case pending which would take one-
third of our yearly appropriation if approved by the State.
This means that $20,000 of the total budget would be ex-
pended on this one person alone which in this case is for a
two month hospitalization at New England Medical Cen-
ter. This particular hospital receives a $267.00 per diem
rate whereas Burbank Hospital and Leominster Hospital re-
ceive $146.00 per diem rate. However, it is sometimes
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent
necessary to send aveteran to Boston for the required medi-
cal care which is not available locally.
The Veterans' Administration questionnaires have been sent out and processed as usual. This year, regardless
of age, all pensioners (even those over 70 years of age) are required to fill out the questionnaire and return same
to the Veterans' Administration. The Veterans' Agent's office is located in the Memorial Building, but any person
unable to reach him at the office may call him at his home. The Veterans' Agent again wishes to thank all Town
officials who assisted his Department during his hospitalizations and illness.

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
During 1978, there were 72 Plumbing Permits and 55
Gas Permits issued as follows: 29 Plumbing Permits for new
homes; 30 Plumbing Permits for remodeled homes; 13 Plumb-
ing Permits for commercial buildings; 4 Gas Permits for new
homes; 5 Gas Permits for commercial uildings; 46 Gas Per-
mits for various gas appliances.
The cooperation of the Building Inspector is greatly
appreciated in our effort to establish that good plumbing is
a big step to a healthy and sanitary environment.
WHY THE PLUMBING INSPECTOR HAS A HEAVY RE-
SPONSIBILITY: While the plumbing industry and I hold the
profession of Plumbing Inspector in high esteem, he is a
much underrated and overlooked individual in State, County
and Municipal government. The plumber protects the health
of the nation; the Plumbing Inspector sees to it that the
plumber does his job in the most professional manner possi-
ble. On the shoulders of each and every Plumbing Inspector
rests aheavy responsibility in termsof the safety and health
of millions of people and also just treatment for millions of
Thomas E. Mortis
consumer's pocketbooks.
Let US not overlook the significance of the Plumbing Inspector's role in terms of consumer protection, particu-
larly in this present decade which is more alive to issues of consumer protection than any other in recent times. If
a man's home is his castle, it is also the single most important investment he makes in his lifetime and a very essen-
tial component of that castle, in terms of possible repair costs, is in the plumbing system. I predict that in the age
of the consumer, we are going to find the searchlight of public interest and public inquiry focused on the role of the
Plumbing Inspector to adegree which perhaps we have never seen before. The Plumbing Inspector has a duty to pro-
tect the public against poor workmanship and to alert the public of any hidden dangers to safety and health.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
In accordance with Section 41, Chapter 98, Gen-
eral Laws, all inhabitants or persons having usual places
of business within the Town using weighing devices for sell-
ing goods, wares, or merchandise for public weighing or
for hire or reward, are notified to bring them in to be re-
tested, adjusted and sealed.
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truck meters, other
commercially used weighing/measuring devices are regu-
larly tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced
spot checks are frequently made. Of the weighing and
TYPE
measuring devices inspected in 1978, 67 were found to be
correct and sealed, 7 scales and pumps were adjusted, and
5 were not sealed.
Lunenburg residents have the right to expect that
whatever they buy is measured or weighed accurately --
to assure this accuracy is the responsibility of the Inspec-
for of Weights and Measures.
Your Inspector extends his appreciation to various
establishments in Town for the cooperation rendered.
Wayne A. Balcom

Conservation Commission
In October of 1978 the Conservation Commission held a dedication ceremony to honor one of its members,
Harold D. Harley. The following was read at this dedication ceremony:
"DEDICATION TO HAROLD HARLEY"
"The Conservation Commission extends a warm welcome to our honored guest, Harold D.
Harley, and his wife Margaret, and a good afternoon to our invited guests. Knowing full
well that our honored guest does not like pomp and circumstance, we will keep this dedi-
cation ceremony short and simple.
We meet under these tall oaks, by agranite marker, to dedicate an area long admired and
loved by Harold. An area such as this is usually described on topo maps, property maps,
and deeds as a swamp, a bog, a marsh or a lowland, It brings to people's minds such
words as dismal, dreary, eerie, or even scary. To the true conservationist it is just the
opposite.
In spring, it is the cradle of life, a place of new birth, an ever-renewing fountain in the
chain of natural development.
In summer, it is an area of protection used by all types of wildlife, from the lowly sala-
mander, to that most graceful of animals, the white-tailed deer.
In the fall, it is a region of peace and serenity as nature winds down from a busy year of
rearing a new generation of plants and animals.
In the winter, it is a haven, a resting place, a deep warm bed that nurtures and protects
life during the frosty time, ready to face another year.
Mulpus Swamp is a classic lowland, fed by a strong and dependable stream. Through the
foresight of Harold and his contemporaries, the Town purchased this land. It encompasses
approximately 300 acres. It will be protected for all time for the enjoyment of our de-
scendants.
These are the reasons this site was chosen to honor a man who isknown notonly in Lunen-
burg; but also statewide, as Mr. Conservation,
HARLEY
HAROLD D.
VISION ARY
OYSER\ ACTONIST
1978
Granite Marker at Harley Wild Life Area
- 26 -
===PAGE 29===
The use of a granite marker signifies the ruggednessof spirit exemplified by Harold Harley
in his quest for conservation land during the past twenty-five years.
The location, you will notice, is under these tall oaks that are a symbol of long life.
Harold has been fortunate in attaining this long life and has shared with the citizens of
Lunenburg the wisdom and knowledge gathered during his eighty-seven years.
We salute you, Harold Harley, and wish you many more yearsof productive and enjoyable
life. We also thank his gracious wife, Margaret, who in her own right has earned the
love and respect of her fellow Lunenburgers.
I want to thank my fellow members on the Commission for their help and support and I
especially want to thank Douglas Fleming and the Greenthumb students for sprucing up
the area and planting the laurel that flank the granite marker. The laurel, by the way,
were donated by Russell Harvey of Lakeview Nurseries.
Someone, of course, had to originate the idea of this dedication. Someone had to provide
the driving force to carry it to its conclusion. That person, of course, is the heart of the
Conservation Commission, Nancy Sholl. Thank you Nancy."
Conservation Commission: Seated - Chairman Raymond G. Chapdelaine
and Harold D. Harley. Standing - left to right - Nancy I. Sholl, John C.
Lahti, Edward H. Hall, Jr., David Richards and Russell N. Harvey.

Extension Service
Extension Service: Seated left to right - Dot Connell, Director Lenore MacCracken
and Judy Smith. Standing left to right - Joyce Downey, Myra Holzer and Dixie Beck.
The year 1978 proved to be a very active, interesting and productive year for our 4-H Clubs. The two Clubs
in existence today are the 4-H Outdoor Adventure Club under the leadership of Joyce Downey and Dot Connell, and
the Bridle Path 4-H Club with Dixie Beck and Judy Smith as leaders, and Donna Camber as assistant leader.
The 4-H Outdoor Adventure Club was launched three years ago, and its main objectives are to help members ap-
preciate and enjoy the beauty and magnitude of our physical world, while learning the responsibilities and obligations
of maintaining it. One of the main activities of the Club has been hiking, Members made their own backpacks,
planned the types of foods and other items necessary for hikes and then trekked off and explored nature at such sites
as Purgatory Chasms, Cowdrey Nature Center, Wapack Trail, Willard Brook, Mt. Wachusett, Leominster State For-
est and Lexington National Park. Other Club activities were night walking in the woods, star viewing, kite design-
ing, constructing and flying, and camping. The 1978 4-H Outdoor Adventure Club members are: Marjorie Paquin,
Anne Cormier, Michelle Hood, Kathy Cornell and Maura Downey and with Susan Downey as assistant leader,
The Bridle Path 4-H members have had the opportunity this year to participate in many varied horse-related
activities. The Club was fortunate to have Ms. Sue Houghton attend its first meeting and present a Tack Care Dem-
onstration with a detailed question and answer period following the presentation. The Club attended a Horse Judg-
ing Clinic at the University of Massachusetts, and another clinic was held at Smithfield Farm in Lunenburg where
Mrs. Gail McAlpine and associates of Littleton, Mass., demonstrated showing in halter. Four Club members demon-
strated Western and English pleasure riding for the group, which also included guests from the Leominster, Westmin-
ster and Sterling 4-H Clubs, The riders were judged and placed by the audience, and then corrected and helped by
Mrs. McAlpine, an expert equestrian. At another Club meeting, a most interesting and informative discussion was
given by Mrs. Patti Cutting Hebert, a former 4-H'er on "How to Prepare for Horse Shows". The Club also held a
records meeting to help our members up-date their individual records. The combined Town 4-H Clubs had an out-
ing via a Town Bus Trip to the Transportation Museum at Larz Anderson Park and the Boston Mounted Police Stable.
A great time was had by all!
- 28 -
===PAGE 31===
Special "fun" activities wereheld this year which included a sleighride at the Silver Ranch, Jaffrey, N. H.,
a Poker Ride through the woods with the Leominster Ride-On 4-H Club, a "Paint" Party for our members and their
families to beautify the ring used at the North Leominster Rod and Gun Club for the Club's horse shows, and our an-
nual Christmas Party which was highlighted by a wonderful hayride on the Stockwell's mule drawn hay wagon.
The Annual Shot Clinic was arranged by Dixie Beck with Dr. Fitzgerald administering the shots. This clinic
is open to all Lunenburg residents who own horses. A special Teeth Floating Clinic by Mr. Harry Robinson of Con-
necticut was also arranged by Dixie Beck for our Club members. Club members were fortunate this year to be able
to participate at several Schooling Shows which were held at the North Leominster Rod & Gun Club. These shows
proved to be very beneficial for the riders, and the Club is very grateful to the many Townspeole who helped spon-
sor them.
In addition, a pre-training course was given to severalof our Club members, and an Incentive Test Preparation
session was planned and led by Donna Camber. We are proud of the many boys and girls who thus went on to pass
their tests on the various levels, and to those members who passed the advanced portion of the tests and went on to the
State Bowl. Several Club members gave their visual presentations at the Turkey Hill Middle School in conjunction
with a Pot Luck Supper which was organized by Mrs. Lenore MacCracken and assisted by Dean MacCracken and Peter
Oulevey.
Several members also went further on to area, county and state levels of competition. One of our members,
Joseph Forkey, received the second place award in the 4-H Competition held at the Eastern States Exposition in
Springfield, Mass. Joey had won first place in the area and county, and second place in the state prior to the
competition at Eastern States. This honor was a first for our Club, and we are very proud of Joey. Another first for
our Club was the second place won by Peter Oulevey for the Massachusetts 4-H judging team, and he then went on
to represent Massachusetts in Harrisburg, Pennsylvania.
Highlighting 1978 was the Club's participation in Lunenburg's 250th Anniversary Celebration, as result of the
organization carried out by the MacCracken family. On Woodsmen's Day in June, a Schooling Horse Show was held
and judged by Bev Saari of Ashby. In August several of our Club members participated in the presentation of the
250th Anniversary Pageant "Under the Buttonwood Tree". In October many members the pleasure of riding in the
250th Anniversary Parade.
The newly elected officers of the Lunenburg Bridle Path 4-H Club are: President - Dean MacCracken; Vice-
President Lisa Banks; Secretary - Renee Womble; Treasurer and Junior Leader - Karin Anderson. In addition, Dean
has been responsible for reporting Club events to the Horseman's Yankee Pedlar. At our Awards Night which was held
in May, 100% attendance was awarded to the following members: Dean MacCracken, Rosemary Smith, Peter Oulevey
and Jo Lynn Beck.
Two 4-H Open Horse Showswere held this year and were excellently managed by Mrs. Ruth Anderson and Mrs.
Judy Smith. These shows help raise money for camperships for our Bridle Path 4-H members and also for the yearly
4-H functions. Twelve boys and girls attended the Southern New England 4-H Horse Camp in Spencer, Mass. this
summer, and we would like to thank all the local merchants and individuals who helped through their sponsorships.
The 1978 Lunenburg Bridle Path 4-H Club members are: Karin Anderson, Lisa Banks, Jo Lynn Beck, Mary
Cassidy, JoAnn Champagne, Ann Marie Forkey, Joseph Forkey, Lori Harris, Lori Heon, Tina Heon, Jonathan Holzer,
Michele Holzer, Melissa Kielty, Dean MacCracken, Rhonda Marceau, Michele Padula, Nicole Sanborn, Jennifer
Sanderson, Marsha Scott, Pamela Scott, Rosemary Smith, Mary Thiel and Renee Womble.
We look forward to an equally successful and further expanding program in the 1979 calendar year.

THE MUNICIPAL FINANCE PROBLEM
"For Every Complex Situation There is a Simple Answer and it is Always Wrong."
The graphs on the preceding page demonstrate that the major financial problem in our municipality and in cities
and towns throughout Massachusetts is OVERRELIANCE ON THE LOCAL PROPERTY TAX to pay for local, State and
Federal services. That overreliance, rather than overspending, is the real problem is demonstrated by the following
comparison based on Fiscal Year 1976 U. S. Census Bureau data:
FISCAL YEAR 1976
PER CAPITA EXPENDITURE PROPERTY TAX PER CAPITA
FIFTY STATE AVERAGE
$ 791.00
$ 266.00
MASSACHUSETTS CITY AND TOWN AVERAGE
789.00
$ 431.00
LUNENBURG AVERAGE
$ 606.20
$ 311.46
The circle graphs on the preceding page also call attention to the even more alarming fact that in the Town of
Lunenburg, due primarily to underfunding of State Mandated Programs, during the last two years alone (from FY 1976
to FY 1978) State Aid as a source of local revenue has decreased from 30.9% to 24.6% while the property tax has
increased from 52.2% to 57.7%. Our graphs also illustrate that throughout the Nation in FY 1976, the last comparison
period for which statistics are available, Federal and State Aid (including sales and income taxes allotted to local
governments) provided 47.4%, or close to one-half of all local revenue, while the corresponding figure for all
Massachusetts cities and towns was 35.6% and for the Town of Lunenburg, 35. 1% nearer to one-third of all local
revenue. In FY 1978 Lunenburg's percentage dropped way down to 29.7%, with shrinking state funding between
FY 1976 and FY 1978 accounting for an estimated 10 of the 49.65 dollars which made up Lunenburg's FY 1979 Tax
Rate. Ten tax rate dollars means approximately $250 to a Lunenburg Taxpayer with an assessed value of $25,000
and even doubles that figure ($500) for a substantial number of Lunenburg home owners. PRETTY SHODDY TREAT-
MENT BY OUR STATE WOULDN'T YOU SAY?
However, this is not to say that at the local level we should not helpbring about general property tax relief by
attempting to reduce municipal expenditures and to this end our operations are being reviewed, alternatives ana-
lyzed and changes introduced and evaluated. It must, of course, be understood that even holding the line is trans-
formed by inflation into losses in purchasing power, losses which are not easily offset even when every possible effi-
ciency is introduced into municipal government.
Unfortunately, efficiencies take time to implement and are not, therefore, immediately visible on the tax rate
and, of course, aren't visible at all when the State reneges on its commitments thereby counteracting any benefits
which would otherwise be derived. Another costly oversight in today's frenzied tax cutting atmosphere is apt to be
recognition of the fact that some efficiencies, including many of the most promising, may initially require higher
costs as current revenues are invested in future returns rather than in current service delivery. Therefore, whereas
we commend the Governor for vigorously attacking the Property Tax dilemma, WE DO NOT INTEND TO BE FORCED
INTO CUT BACK TACTICS AIMED AT SHORT TERM ECONOMIES DESIGNED AROUND THE FULFILLMENT OF A
CAMPAIGN PROMISE!
In any cut back, administrative services are particularly vulnerable because they are not usually supported by a
vocal clientor citizen group. Some administrative areas can be cut butothers need to be strengthened if the goal of
minimizing service reductions, while spending less, is to be achieved. Fortunately Lunenburg voters, officials, and
Finance Committee people are much more aware of this fact than are the corresponding groups in many cities and
towns. As a resultof this awareness, comparatively speaking, our financial house is in exceedingly good order. For
example, more funds are provided for tax collections than in most of the surrounding towns and better tax collections
have permitted accounts receivable to be converted into fluid assets. Funds have also been provided for vigorous
cash management of all monies collected. As a result, in FY 1979, a conservatively estimated $100,000 will be
earnedon invested funds. (See page 68 for breakdown of investments.) Here again we serve notice on our Governor,
our legislators and any other State entity which attempts to in any way undercut or take away the financial advan-
tages which we have carefully built up
HANDS OFF !!!
- 34 -
===PAGE 37===
In conclusion, our local effort to lower property taxes is being based on the principle of limiting local taxes
in combination with increased State Aid, (no strings to be attached), and in this regard we are in agreement with
the Governor and would concede that the Governor's bill is an important step in that direction. Nonetheless, it is
felt that municipal tax levy and spending caps as proposed, translate into types of service cuts which will not be
acceptable to the average Lunenburg citizen. A concentrated local effort is being made to reduce expenditures
through more efficient management, and we feel this can best be accomplished with little or no intervention by the
State. That is to say, the State should effectively "get off of our backs" and shift its emphasis to putting its own
house in order, a monumental task which would reap far-reaching benefits for the State, for the Communities and
most of all for the overburdened Property Owner.
TOWN OFFICE STATISTICAL REPORTS
The Town Office combines the offices of the Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer and includes all account-
ing functions. This arrangement, which permits US to serve the public more competently, is one example of reducing
expenditures through more efficient management. It allows the utilization of personnel more effectively, by chan-
neling their energies in different capacities as peak workloads occur in one or another of the several departments.
Following are statistical reports of the Town Treasurer for the last full fiscal year, FY 1978, and a reconcil-
iation of the Treasurer's cash as of December 31, 1978, the end of the last calendar year; a report of the Tax Col-
lector for FY 1978; and extracts from the Town Clerk's official vital records and town meeting and election minutes.

ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on April 2, 1977 to be effective the beginning of
the payroll week following July 1, 1977, and substituting therefor the Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel
Board and appearing in the report of the Finance Committee, such action to be effective the beginning of the payroll week following
July 1, 1978, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY THAT THE TOWN AMEND ARTICLE XV OF THE TOWN'S BY-
LAWS ENTITLED "SALARY ADMINISTRATION PLAN OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG" BY DELETING THE CLASSIFICATION AND
SALARY SCHEDULE VOTED ON APRIL 2, 1977 TO BE EFFECTIVE THE BEGINNING OF THE PAYROLLWEEK FOLLOWING JULY 1,
1977, AND SUBSTITUTING THEREFOR THE CLASSIFICATION AND SALARY SCHEDULE PROPOSED BY THE PERSONNEL COM-
MITTEE AND APPEARING ON PAGE 9 AND PAGE 10 OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT, WITH THE EXCEPTION THAT THE
DESIGNATIONS "SUPERVISING NURSE", "SUPERVISING NURSE WITH BACHELOR OF SCIENCE IN PUBLIC HEALTH", "VISITING
NURSE" AND "SUBSTITUTE NURSE" UNDER THE CATEGORY "ADMINISTRATIVE AND PROFESSIONAL" BE EXCLUDED FROM
- 74 -

SAID SCHEDULE, SUCH ACTION TO BE EFFECTIVETHE BEGINNING OF THE PAYROLLWEEK FOLLOWING JULY 1, 1978, AND
THAT TWO ADDITIONAL STEPS BE ADDED TO THE CEMETERY SUPERINTENDENT CATEGORY AS FOLLOWS: $8,595, $9, 110.
ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, all sums
of money necessary to defray the charges and expenses of the Town for Fiscal Year 1979 and to fix the salaries and compensation of
all elected officers of the Town and any other items included in the budget of the Finance Committee, or act anything thereon.
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY ON MOTION OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE THAT THE BUDGET BE TAKEN UP AT THIS TIME AND
THAT THE TOWN APPROPRIATE THOSE SPECIFIC SUMS OF MONEYRECOMMENDED IN THE LAST COLUMN OF THE BUDGET
APPEARING ON PAGES 2, 3 AND 4 OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT, AND THAT TO MEET SUCH APPROPRIATION
$134,034 BE APPROPRIATED FROM SURPLUS REVENUE, $38,673 BE APPROPRIATED FROM THE ANTI-RECESSION GRANT FOR
CARE OF THE MUNICIPAL REFUSE DISPOSAL AREA, $65,263 BE APPROPRIATED FROM REVENUE SHARING TO PAY FOR MEM-
BERSHIP IN THE NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH AND UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION CHARGES, $3,551
BE APPROPRIATED FROM THE MACHINERY RENTAL ACCOUNT FOR OPERATING, PURCHASING AND REPAIRING ROAD MA-
CHINERY, $34,597 BE APPROPRIATED FROMOVERLAY SURPLUS TO THERESERVEFUND, AND THE REMAINDER OF THE TOTAL
BUDGET VOTED BE RAISED BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATED, AND THAT TO SUPPLEMENT AMOUNTS APPROPRIATED UN-
DER THE BUDGET, THE TOWN APPROVE THE APPROPRIATION OF $3,065.63 FROM MONIES RECEIVED FROM THE STATE AS
AID TO LIBRARIES AND $1,447.33 FROM THE DOG REFUND RECEIVED FROM THE COUNTY FOR LIBRARY PURPOSES, AND
$5,001.05 RECEIVED FROM CEMETERY TRUST FUND INCOME FOR CEMETERY PURPOSES, WITH THE FOLLOWING AMEND-
MENTS: ITEM #7-TREASURER'S SALARY-WAS INCREASED TO $12,200.00; ITEM #27-POLICE DEPARTMENT-WAS INCREASED
TO $168,826.00; ITEM #69-CEMETERIES-WAS INCREASED TO $10,634.00.

QUESTION 1 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of on amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and Senate on May 28, 1975, by a vote of 220-53, and on September 7,
1977, by a vote of 243-20?
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment would permit the legislature to establish as many as four different classes
of real property for tax purposes. Property in any one class would be required to be assessed, rated and taxed proportionately but
property in different classes could be assessed, rated and taxed differently. The legislature could grant reasonable exemptions.
The constitution presently requires all property (other than wild lands, forest lands, and certain agricultural and horticultural lands)
to be assessed and rated equally at full value for tax purposes.
VOTE:
YES - 1025
NO - 1757
BLANKS - 134
QUESTION 2 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and Senate on May 28, 1975, by a vote of 267-3, and on August 10, 1977,
by a vote of 250-1?
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment would allow a governor who had not served in the preceding year as
governor to submit a proposed budget to the legislature within eight weeks of the beginning of the legislative session. A governor
who had served in the preceding year would still be required to submit a proposed budget within three weeks of the beginning of a
legislative session.
VOTE:
YES - 1779
NO - 861

Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
by a vote of 257-0?
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and Senate on August 11, 1976, by a vote of 248-5, and on May 24, 1978,
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment would give the legislature the power to establish a different method of
property taxation for land which is used for recreational purposes and for land preserved in its natural state. It would add these
two categories to the existing constitutional provision which allows the legislature to tax wild and forest land differently. The
amendment's stated purpose is to develop and conserve natural resources and the environmental benefits of recreational land.

January 1, 1978
A PROCLAMATION
WHEREAS, the Town of Lunenburg was incorporated as a town in 1728, and
WHEREAS, voters at the Annual Town Meeting of 1973 approved the formation of the
250th Anniversory Committee to plan a celebration in observance of the 250th
anniversary of incorporation as a town:
NOW, THEREFORE, We, the Board of Selectmen of the Town of Lunenburg, acting in our
official capacity, do hereby proclaim, declare, and make known that the
year of 1978 be known as the 250th Anniversary Year of the Town of
Lunenburg.
AND WE HEREBY enjoin upon the people of Lunenburg to take an active role in the
observance of the anniversary by clothing themselves in the manner and custom
of our early inhabitants when participating in anniversary events; by joining
in all activities planned for the observance; and by attending all special
ceremonies and programs during the anniversary year.
IN WITNESS THEREOF, We have hereunto set our hands, and caused the seal of the Town
of Lunenburg to be affixed hereto.
Done in the Town of Lunenburg, the first day of January in the year of our
Lord, nineteen hundred and seventy-eight; and of the independence of the
United States of America the two hundred and second; and of the incorporation
of the Town the two hundred and fiftieth.
Rudolph Masella, Chairman

Sherwood G. Winchester, Member
