- 85 -
===PAGE 88===
250th Anniversary Committee
1728.LUNENBURG
NENBURG.
MASS:
250th Anniversary Committee in front of Commemorative Arch on the Center Common erected as part of the 250th
Celebration. The Committee - (left to right) - Margaret P. Harley, William R. Proctor, Jean E. Hobbs, Chairman
Raymond G. MacCracken, Robert J. Ebersole, Mary E. Bingham, Muriel J. Francis and David L. Porter.
HAPPY BIRTHDAY, DEAR LUNENBURG, HAPPY BIRTHDAY TO YOU!!
These words and a gigantic birthday cake were the closing scenes of the Pageant, and the final unit of the
Parade. In both instances, everyone joined in the singing and the cheers in tribute to our Town -- Lunenburg --
250 years old. What a year -- from the opening ceremony to the Ball -- and a good time was had by all !!
On January 1st, at noon on the Center Common, Rudolph Masella, Chairman of the Board of Selectmen, read
the Proclamation designating 1978 as the 250th Anniversary year. A Winter Carnival on February 4th and 5th was
complete with snow sculptures, snowmobile races, and a dance. With an appropriate ceremony on Arbor Day, April
28th, a buttonwood free was planted on the Center Common, and the Boston Post Cane was presented to the Town's
oldest resident, Mrs. Mabel Barrett.
A Woodsmen's Day at the Sportsman's Club on June 17th, was followed by the July 3rd Bonfire and 18th Army
Band Concert, and by the Firemen's Muster on July 9th. Throughout the year, many organizations conducted events
in conjunction with the 250th Anniversary contributing to the observance.
On August 1st, the actual date of Incorporation as a Town in 1728, Alumni Park was dedicated, cannons were
fired and church bells rung to mark the formal opening of Old Home Week. In a program heldon the Lower Common,
the original Article of Incorporation was read by the Chairman of the Boardof Selectmen, Sherwood G. Winchester,
and an old-time band concert was presented by the Town Band. Tour Day on August 2nd, with narrated bus tours
and a luncheon, featured visits to the three oldest houses in the Community, as well as to the Upper Town Hall and
the Historical Society. Whalom Park saluted the 250th Anniversary with a Lunenburg Day on August 3rd, with that
day's events concluding with a Block Dance and dance exhibit in Lunenburg Center. Recreation Day was observed
on Saturday, August 5th. During Old Home Week, a Hospitality Booth was in operation in front of the Ritter Mem-
orial Library, manned by volunteers, A guest book for visitors was maintained and souvenir items were for sale.
- 86 -
===PAGE 89===
The highlight of Old Home Week was the pageant, "Under the Buttonwood Tree", performed on August 4th and
5th in the High School Auditorium, with a cast and crew of over 150. Church services on Sunday, August 6th, were
followed by family picnics, a concert by the Westminster Brass Ensemble, a parachute jump, and climaxed with the
"best fireworks ever" on the grounds at the High School.
The 250th Anniversary Parade on October 1st with its many bands and floats, was one of the biggest and best
organized of any community in the area. The Grand Ball on October 8th at the Wallace Civic Center, complete
with Phyllis Diller and Skitch Henderson, was a fitting closing to the year's activities.
Noneof these eventswould have been possible without the work of many people, and it is not possible to name
them all. Special tribute must be paid to the talents of Marjorie Lucier, author of "Under the Buttonwood Tree", and
to Janet Cragin, its Director, togetherwith the many other people who contributed to the production; to Joseph Cro-
nin, Chairman, and Margaret Bachmann of the Parade Committee; and to Francis McCarthy, President, and Donald
Critchley of the Rotary Club, sponsors of the Ball.
One of the major accomplishments of the 250th Anniversary observance was the publication of the Town his-
tory "Lunenburg - The Heritage of Turkey Hills", co-authored by Nelde K. Drumm and Margaret P. Harley. An-
other permanent remembrance is the 250th Anniversary Quilt, with squares contributed by Lunenburg needlewomen,
and constructed under the direction of Dondi Larue, assisted by Barbara Pease.
Other accomplishments of the 250th Anniversary Committee since being appointed in 1973 include the mount-
ing of date plaques on houses built in the 1700's; placing granite markers at homesite of Samuel Page, the buttonwood
tree on Buttonwood Place, and the new buttonwood tree on the Center Common; erection of the Commemorative Arch
and Town Seal on the Center Common; and reviving of the July 3rd Bonfire and Independence Day observance.
A scrapbook is being compiled of pictures, programs, newspaper articles and other information pertaining to
the 250th Anniversary celebration. With permission of the Townspeople, this, together with the 250th Anniversary
Quilt, will be placed in the collection of the Historical Society. A movie of the pageant has been donated to the
Historical Society by Dr. Franklin Holzer, and a video tape of the parade produced and placed at the Ritter Memor-
ial Library. Both are available on loan to Town organizations.
To finalize 250th projects, a T. G. I. O. Dance is scheduled for February 3rd at the Lunenburg Sportsman's
Club. The T. G. 1. O. stands for "Thank God It's Over", an honest feeling on the part of the Committee, for five
years is a long time to be planning and preparing for a Townwide celebration. On the other hand, missed will be the
togetherness and the sense of Town pride that was generated by the activities. Hopefully, the 250th Anniversary
Committee has kindled a spark that will last at leastuntil 2028, when Lunenburg is 300 years old, and another Com-
mittee is directing the celebration. The following is the 250th Anniversary Committee Treasurer's report submitted
by Robert J. Ebersole:

No-School Signals-1978-79
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of No-School Signals will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE
STATION INDICATES NO SCHOOL AT THE FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE
GROUPS INDICATED:
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5
10:00 AM
P. M. Session/Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of Radio Stations WEIM and WFGL,
Fitchburg; WLMS, Leominster, as well as WTAG and WSRS (Stereo 96) in Worcester; and WBZ in Boston,
as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone calls
as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center or bus
contractors might not be notified on time. Parents can help by not allowing their children to make
unnecessary calls. Your cooperation and understanding will be appreciated by all concerned.
Report of Attendance Officer
During the 1978 calendar year attendance responsibilities have been the responsibility of Mr.
Walter J. Dubzinski, Jr., Mr. John F. Thibeault, and Mrs. Page-Ann Coleman.

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my First Annual
Report as Superintendent of Schools in Lunenburg.
This report covers the year ending December 31,
1978.
As you read the reports of the other admin-
istrators and principals, you will receive a
more detailed accounting of the events that have
taken place in the last year because my personal
observations and intimate knowledge of this
school system can be only for a few months.
Although a major portion of my report will be a
"look to the future," I will attempt to relate
to you some background information and data of
some of the events and decisions that have taken
place.
LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS
WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D.
Unquestionably, good schools are definitely
inherent in Lunenburg's reputation. They are
good schools because of the students who attend
them; the teachers who teach here; the parents who support them financially and with personal
involvement; the members of the school committee who put in long hours and personal commitment to
honest and open discussions; the administrators who care and give of themselves beyond what is
normally expected of them; the custodial and clerical staff who approach their jobs as caring and
willing professionals; and the cafeteria workers who sustain us daily. All of these factors,
collectively and individually, contribute to the quality we so proudly share. As Superintendent
of reputation. Schools, I share in this pride but only with the realization of what others contribute to this
SOME CHANGES
Since my arrival in August, I have been involved in two major aspects of my position:
(1) to help teachers and administrators to complete the grade reorganization process
that had been started before I came; and,
(2) to gain as much knowledge of the school system as soon as I could and to put my-
self in a relatively short period of time in a position to present to the School
Committee the FY-80 Budget.
The transition to any new position is never really easy but this one was complicated by a
major grade reorganization and preparing a budget in a year that fiscal austerity and financial
concerns were very obvious.
GRADE REORGANIZATION
Much can be said about this decision and the planning that went into this effort. Suffice to
say here that I applaud the citizens of Lunenburg for their support of this plan. Educationally
it has improved every aspect of our overall program. The success of the reorganization belongs to
the Principals, Dr. Goodrich, the staff, and in particular to Bob Okerman and his staff, who did
all of the physical remodeling and movement of material. I will be ever grateful to all who par-
ticipated in this most successful and smooth reorganization.
- 104 -
===PAGE 107===
NEW PROGRAMS
With grade reorganization came late busing and the "after school" program at the MIDDLE
SCHOOL. This has been a most successful and popular program.
The efforts of Mr. Gillis and his
staff in implementing this program and
making it a great success deserves the
congratulations of the entire community.
For this report, I will include the
results of the first-term of this pro-
gram:

ASSISTANT SUPERINTENDENT OF SCHOOLS -
ADMINISTRATIVE ASSISTANT TO PRINCIPAL OF MIDDLE SCHOOL
In December, the School Committee approved a proposal that I presented to realign some
positions. Dr. Burton Goodrich has now been named Assistant Superintendent of Schools and will
expand his position and responsibilities to include many aspects that are now necessary to respond
to in today's complex and demanding educational scene.
To replace Dr. Goodrich at the Middle School, Mr. Richard Garvey has been appointed Admin-
istrative Assistant. This will give the Middle School two strong, capable and highly professional
administrators. I am most grateful to the School Committee for their approval of these positions
and I am most pleased by the quality of the two individuals.
SOME DIFFICULTIES
In his 1975 Report of the Superintendent of Schools, Dr. William R. Holland wrote:
"Does anticipated enrollment decline mean any organizational changes in our current
grade structure and building utilization? Does it mean a cut-back in staff?"
(Answer) "Yes. A serious look should be made of enrollment patterns prior to the
1978-79 school year. If conditions remain stable and Lunenburg's pupil
enrollment continues to gradually decline leveling 066 slightly above
2000 students, I would recommend reorganizing the school system into a
grade K-5, 6-8 and 9-12 organizational plan. Under this plan grade five
would occupy the Annex. Another alternative would be simply move the
eighth grade back into the Middle School converting that school into a
grade 5-8 building; however, this alternative may not be feasible number-
wise until the early 1980's (I remind you that we accomplished this
reorganization this year). It is also expected that with enrollment
decline some teaching staff will be cut."
I feel that back in 1975, Dr. Holland was predicting some difficult and emotional decisions
would have to be made in the next few years. In 1978-79 we were faced with some hard and rather
obvious data:
(1) In 1975-76, our K-4 school population was 742. In 1979-80, we are projecting a
K-4 population of 600.
(2) Our projected kindergarten enrollments are as follows:
School Years

It is worthy to note that we are this year (1978-79) already 16 students below what was
projected.
In the meantime we were projected to have a total school population for 1978-79 of 2049 stu-
dents. We presently have 2008. The high school presently has 705 students and is projected to

this information means that we are in a community with declining enrollment and in a state that
reflects a similar decline. While we have been declining in student enrollment, we have not been
sacrificing our class size ratios as the following data indicates:

*Because Lunenburg follows the practice of establishing one class at grade levels one
through six as a transitional class (development grades 1-3, skills grades 4-6, with
maximum class size at 15 to 17) sizes among the other classes at these grade levels are
actually higher than averages indicated.
My one concern here is that we should not allow the class sizes at the Middle School to
increase and should develop some plans to reallocate staff to further reduce a 1:28 ratio in this
school.
Our recommended personnel cuts this year were particularly very difficult because we were
faced with the elimination of qualified and respected members of our educational community. The
decision for staff elimination and consolidation of classes rests with the Superintendent. I
assure you that these decisions were made with every possible avenue explored and evaluated. The
decision for staff cut-backs are never done simply to find a dollar reduction in the budget.
They are considered in light of curriculum and student needs. We will continue to monitor our
staff needs over the next few years and hopefully we can do this as a result of long-range
planning.
Teaching the gifted and demanding student who attend our schools requires many of the
qualities of a great editor, working hard, often selflessly, for each student, bending one's pride
and submerging one's ego to the "amour propre" found in luxuriant abundance in each student. To
bring out the best of talent requires great talent as well as tact and will power. Lecturing at,
teaching to, describe the process poorly. The pace must be maintained and the exactness of
measurements of the precision of meaning insisted upon, but the task is essentially to draw out,
to encourage formulations, and to discover the fullness of implications in a paragraph, phrase,
or word. Too many educational programs fail simply because the teachers are neither talented nor
well-educated. The faculty in Lunenburg is composed of well-educated men and women who have
proven or must prove their ability to draw out the best in their students as well as demand hard
work of them. What use gifted students if the teachers are not gifted?

FUTURE CONSIDERATIONS
In this portion of my report, I will try to indicate some of the goals that we would like to
address in the next few years.
1. Minimum competency;
2. K-12 curriculum evaluation and articulation;
3. Scheduling and semester offerings at the High School;
4. Reallocation of present staff to affect greater flexibility
and usage;
5. In-service programs to increase teacher-effectiveness in
classroom;
6. Five-year plan for updating our facilities in the areas of
repairs and compliance to handicap laws;
7. Greater involvement with Monty Tech for possibilities with
our students;
8. Increased concentration on basic skill development for our
students;
9. Improve the use of our school grounds with involvement with
Parks and Recreation; and
10. In line with fiscal constraints and budgetary limitations,
seek to find methods of better utilization of present
resources.
All of these goals and many more that I did not relate require the concentrated effort of
the entire community. Students must give us their input into the necessary directions as to
curriculum, environment and staffing. Parents must continue their involvement in our system and
staff and administration must continue their creativity and maximize their efforts. We feel that
we have a quality school system but we also believe that it can and must be better
not for
ourselves as adults, but for the sake of our students.
CONCLUSION
As I indicated in the beginning of this report, all of you should read the remaining reports
of the school department for a clear and accurate report of the past year. In concluding this
report, I would like to pay special tribute to the members of the School Committee for their
countless hours spent in preparation of material and formulation of policy; my fellow administra-
tors who have shown me the ability to care and understand; to the staff for their talent and pro-
fessionalism; and to the non-teaching staff for their support. Special thanks go to my secretary,
Mrs. "Ginny" Welch, who helped me fumble my way through my first year and to Bob Okerman for all
of his efforts. I wish to thank Dr. Goodrich for his efforts in providing the base to "jump"
forward during this first year. Finally, to the townspeople who continue to support an excellent
school system. What we have in quality is a direct result of your participation and support.

HEALTH EDUCATION
Through the Curriculum Advisory Committee, established by the Superintendent of Schools, a
sub-committee was selected in 1976 to study the health education opportunities offered in the
Lunenburg Public Schools.
The findings indicated a greater need for formal and specific health education instruction
on the secondary level. Although the subject has been taught for many years on the secondary
level, there had been no special syllabus, but merely a "hit or miss" program through the regular
science and social studies courses.
In 1977 a teacher was contracted to coordinate and implement a health education program with
the help and counsel of a Health Education Committee made up of administrators, teachers, nurses,
physicians, and a clergyman.
- 110 -
===PAGE 113===
From this input a pilot instructional program was established last year resulting in a formal
required program being implemented in 1978-79. Presently, there are one hundred eighty-five grade
nine students being taught this course twice a week for the full year. The course of study in-
cludes such topics as values, nutrition, physiology, smoking, alcohol and drugs, communicable
diseases, family living, etc.
It has been recommended that in the future all students graduating from Lunenburg High School
will have been required to pursue this important subject.
The syllabus is available at the school for any interested parent or other citizen.
FACILITY REORGANIZATION
All who read the Superintendent's Annual Report of 1977 are aware of the great amount of
study, work, and effort which went into grade reorganization as a result of declining enrollment
in K - 7 and overcrowding in the high school.
The major effect of this reorganization in the high school has been the removal of the
eighth grade from the student body, and action long overdue because of social, motivational, and
behavioral discrepancies between high school and middle school students. By my observations the
high school student body has been much better behaved and the climate of the school has greatly
improved because of the change. I am sure that the same can be reported by those responsible for
the instructional program of grade eight students.
A minor effect of the reorganization plan has been a needed restructure of the academic
facility locations of the high school which was not possible during overcrowded conditions of the
past. Each subject area department has now been located in its own designated and separate region
within the school.
This restructuring has resulted in the better sharing within departments of common instruc-
tional materials and resources, the sharing of instructional assistance from colleagues with
common academic interests which leads to camaraderie and improved morale, and more effective
supervision and evaluation.
BIO II FIELD TRIP - Cape Cod
GRADUATION REQUIREMENTS
Although Lunenburg High School has always had specific course requirements throughout the
four years and specific standards for each student to meet in order to graduate, these requirements
and standards have now been spelled out more clearly and definitively as recommended by the high
school administrators and administrative council.

===PAGE 114===
The Lunenburg School Committee on December 20, 1978 voted that requirements for graduation
from Lunenburg High School shall be the acquisition of nineteen (19) Carnegie units of credit
which must include the following units:
English.

Science

......
1 Unit (1/4 per year)
Health Education
1/4 Unit
These requirements are understood as having to be successfully completed (passing grades) in
order for the diploma to be granted from Lunenburg High School. This policy is to become effec-
tive with the Class of 1983 (currently grade eight).

CURRICULUM
HEALTH PROGRAM
Although all areas of curriculum are scrutinized and studied every year, the health program
received a great deal of attention in 1978-79.
The purpose of the health program designed for grades six through eight is to provide students
with information which will enable them to make decisions affecting vital areas of their lives
based on knowledge.
Assistance in understanding the decision-making process is provided throughout the program in
hopes that the student may determine a proper value structure for himself/herself prior to
encountering the real-life situation.
Subject areas covered by the program are presented in an organized and cumulative manner
from grades six through eight in the Middle School. Material is presented in the areas of
nutrition, exercise, alcohol, drugs,
smoking, first-aid, and breast self-
examination as well as human sexual-
ity. A continuous values and decis-
ion-making program underscores the
programs as well.
The Middle School program com-
plements the present fifth grade
health program and provides an
excellent introduction to correspond-
ing high school program areas where
the material might be presented in
greater depth.

REPORT OF DIRECTOR OF READING (K-12)
AND LANGUAGE ARTS (K-8)
Since Director of Language Arts is a new
title in this school system, people frequently
ask for a definition of "language arts."
The language arts are usually considered
all the skills needed for sending and receiving
communication. Speaking and writing are the
major divisions under "expressing" or sending
communication. Listening and reading are the
major divisions under receiving communication.
LANGUAGE ARTS
Sending Communication Receiving Communication
ROSE M. FEINBERG, Ed. D.
Speaking
Listening
Writing
Reading
At the middle school and high school, most language arts components are taught during the
"English" period, with greater emphasis on writing, grammar and literature.
Under the general headings of (1) listening, (2) speaking, (3) writing, and (4) reading, many
other skills are developed.
(1) Concentration on listening includes such skills as following directions, meaningful par-
ticipation in discussions and comprehension of oral information.
(2) Concentration on speaking includes such skills as group discussion, appropriate language
usage, clear expression of ideas and other oral communication.
Listening and speaking skills are frequently taught and/or reinforced at the same time
since they are basically dependent on each other in activities.
(3) Concentration on writing includes such skills as written expression of ideas, spelling,
punctuation, vocabulary usage, sentence development, handwriting and other areas of
written communication.
(4) Concentration on reading includes a multitude of skills ranging from recognizing the
alphabet to using reference materials effectively. Since reading involves so many skills,
it is generally treated as a subject with its own heading as well as being considered one
of the language arts.
The emphasis on specific language arts components may vary at different grade levels, but the
relationship between listening, speaking, writing and reading crosses all grades. An experience
with language in one area helps enrich the other areas. For example, better understanding of what
is read takes place when a child has experienced the words in some way previously--seeing the
objects, discussing the idea or even hearing words spoken by others. Through reading, children
broaden their experiences, vocabulary and understandings. Reading can, thus, help improve written
and oral communication.
Although the "language arts" are taught in specific time blocks, they are necessarily part of
all subjects and are reinforced throughout the school day as students speak, listen, read or write
in any subject.
- 121

READING PROGRAM CHANGES

Grade reorganization and scheduling changes affected the reading programs at the elementary,

AT THE ELEMENTARY SCHOOL, a 90-minute language arts block was established for grades three,
and grouping for instruction as before.
The major change is in scheduling when the various language arts are taught. Previously,

AT THE MIDDLE SCHOOL, two new reading pro-
grams were established.
As of September, 1978 all seventh graders
have a daily reading class. Previously, only
those students who were not taking a foreign
language were assigned to reading classes. Since
foreign language instruction now begins in eighth
grade, a program called "Communication Skills"
was designed which would be of value to seventh
graders at all reading skill levels.
COMMUNICATION SKILLS UNITS are designed to
give students reading experiences which involve
"real life" reading materials and topics which
will help them develop the reading skills
necessary in everyday life as well as in their
future schooling.
All seventh grade students will have reading
experiences in the following four units with a
different unit taught each marking term: Con-
sumer Awareness, Career Awareness, Newspaper, and
Dr. Feinberg spends time doing
Research Projects.
Language Arts lessons in classrooms.
CONSUMER AWARENESS: In this unit, the focus is on using reading skills to become knowledge-
able consumers. Among the topics included are: ways to shop; evaluating advertising and labels;
budgeting; borrowing; insurance; consumer rights; banking; taxes; and guarantees.
CAREER AWARENESS: In this unit, the focus is on using reading skills to become aware of the
many career possibilities available in our society. Among the topics included are: career
options; activities related to specific jobs; skills and training required for specific jobs; and
personal career preferences.
NEWSPAPER: In this unit, the focus is on using reading skills to get information from this
important resource. Among the topics included are: general organization of the newspaper; head-
lines; news articles; editorials; classified advertisements; special interest sections; and
responsibilities of people who publish the news.
RESEARCH PROJECTS: In this unit, the focus is on using reference skills to get information
from a variety of sources. Among the topics included are: following directions; notetaking;
organizing information; outlining; using the library and finding reference materials; and using
specific references such as dictionaries, encyclopedias, maps, graphs, tables, filmstrips.
An eighth grade reading program was titled "Life Skills" since the focus is on providing read-
ing experiences which can be applied to real-life situations. Most of the material used in the
three reading classes relate to practical life skills such as reading forms, following directions,
using references, consumer awareness and career information. In addition, students read and dis-
cuss various books.
- 122
===PAGE 125===
Lynn Fletcher, reading specialist at the Turkey Hill Middle School, has played a major part
in developing and implementing these programs.
AT THE HIGH SCHOOL, an elective reading program was developed by Mr. Harry Angevine for stu-
dents in grades 9-12. The program was developed to be of value to students of all reading abili-
ties, providing instruction to improve both basic and advanced reading skills. Due to scheduling
difficulties, not all students who were interested in taking this elective were able to do SO.
Reading at the high school will continue to be offered as an elective to all students.
PARENTS AND READING/LANGUAGE ARTS PROGRAMS
Efforts have been made to keep parents informed about the language arts and reading programs
and to encourage parental involvement at the elementary school.
Newsletter articles have been used as one of the ways to keep parents informed about programs
and issues. Some of the titles of articles in IT'S ELEMENTARY were: "Reading At Home, What's On
The Bulletin Board?, Creative Dramatics In The Language Arts Program, Parent coffees Focus On
Home Activities, using Television For Learning, Summertime Vacation Learning, Books and Magazines
Make Excellent Holiday Gifts."
The school-wide Newsletters have included articles about the definition of language arts, a
description of the seventh-grade reading program, and essays written for a state-wide writing
contest.
Parent Coffees were conducted at both the elementary and middle schools.
The series of coffees at the elementary school were directed to parents of children from pre-
school through grade four. The purposes of this series were: (1) To give parents a better under-
standing of the reading/language development process; (2) To provide specific suggestions for the
types of activities which could be done at home to build needed skills; (3) To answer questions
about the programs in the school; and (4) To provide an opportunity for open communication.
The coffees at the middle school were directed to parents of students in grades five, six and
seven. Focus was on suggesting ways parents could help children improve their language arts
skills.
At each session, materials containing suggestions for home activities were provided to be
taken home. These are available to any parent upon request. Other meetings of this type are
planned.
A Language Arts Volunteer Program was established in the spring of 1978. Barbara Rhoten, a
parent volunteer, became the coordinator of the elementary school language arts volunteers. A
group of parents spent many hours creating additional materials for use in classrooms at the
Passios School. The volunteer program continues, with parents collecting and donating material,
making learning activities at home, and helping in some classrooms. The school language arts pro-
gram has been enriched by parental involvement.
CONCLUSION
The skills a student has in the language arts (reading, writing, speaking, listening) will
contribute to the student's performance in most areas of learning. Why do some children progress
further and faster than others in the language arts areas? There are many influences on a child's
development of language arts skills, including: physical readiness, student ability, background
experiences, personal attitude and school experiences.
There are no guarantees that following any one plan will mean success for a child in school.
The school does much through instruction to build the skills children need in the language arts.
What should not be overlooked, however, is the importance of the home in the development of the
child's abilities.

===PAGE 126===

the foundation for school success.
2. After children enter school ,much learning continues to take place at home as parents spend
time with their children. Parents talking about things, explaining situations, answering
dren. questions and listening can develop the understandings and language skills of their chil-
3. Parents are the models of how what is learned in school is actually used in life outside
school. Children need to see parents read and write.
4. A child's opinion of himself or herself can influence school success. Therefore, it is
important for parents to help children gain confidence about themselves.

Report of Special Needs Director
Special education in this community has continued
to meet the substantial special needs of children and
youth. Such special needs are determined by a school-
centered evaluation team and are "based upon a finding
that a child, because of temporary or more permanent
adjustment difficulties or attributes arising from
intellectual, sensory, emotional or physical factors,
cerebral dysfunctions, perceptual factors, or other
specific learning impairments, or any combination
thereof, is unable to progress effectively in a regular
education program and requires special education.
(Regulations 766,, Massachusetts Department of Education,
September 1978.)
An evaluation team, through assessments and with
information from parents, determines the special needs.
An assigned special needs staff member receives
referrals and coordinates evaluations in each school
building. Parents are active participants in the pro-
cess and have the right to approve or reject the edu-
FLOYD BENITZ
cational plan designed to follow through with listed
services.
By state regulation, we search for children ages three years and older who may have special
needs. The child search for preschoolers is accomplished by census related information, public
announcements and screening services available in the schools. By plan, children with severe and
multiple problems can be reached in the preschool years. However, much of the success of early
identification is dependent upon the referral by parent, physician or other agency since the child
at teacher. age three and four years is oftentimes not yet available to observation by an experienced
During the elementary years, expectations in the child's use of vision, hearing, fine muscle
coordination, thinking and remembering are heightened. Weaknesses in any area of functioning may
- 124 -
===PAGE 127===
be accompanied by or compounded by emotional and social factors. In Lunenburg, it is our goal to
assist the child and family relative to any factors that affect the learning of the child.
Each school has a Resource Room for special needs students. In some respects, the Resource
Room is like the one room school of earlier years. At any given class period, the students may
vary in ages and learning problem. Oftentimes, each child is working at a task individually
planned and may be assisted by another student. Children with problems of a physical, mental,
educational, emotional or social nature may be instructed for an hour a day or most of the day.
The learning activity and the time required are planned for the individual. As a program, the
Resource Room provides an important flexibility in meeting the special needs of children.
Learning disability tutors work with individual students or small groups depending on the
need. This service is available from kindergarten through high school.
Children with some rather severe physical and learning problems have been successfully inte-
grated into our schools this year. The Special Education law has been one important stimulus for
the event. Capable classroom teachers supported by services to the children from the disciplines
of physical therapy, occupational therapy and speech have contributed to this progress in educa-
tion.
Selected middle and high school students are working and studying in PROJECT ENHANCE. It is
the purpose of this project to guide students in the development of good work habits and attitudes
through real work activities with people. The project seeks work assignments within the town that
will help the community or individuals. Interested persons or town agencies may obtain more infor-
mation from Rick Hens at school, 582-7751.
PROJECT GREENTHUMB continues to develop and refine the preparation of young people in work
and study related to horticulture. Some students with special learning needs have earned the
opportunity to continue in this project.
A full-time teacher is now in the high school to identify students in need of services beyond
those regularly available from teachers and counselors. John Thibeault brings his previous exper-
iences as a teacher and as a high school teacher-consultant to this new position. High School
students quitting school are apprised of their rights to an evaluation of their problem but, of
greater importance, they are informed of the opportunity to return to school and complete their
education when they feel ready.
Our Special Needs Educational Collaborative (S. N. E. c.) with offices in Ayer has developed
superior programs for our children who require substantially separate programs. A state review
and visitation team to the Life Skills Program assessed the quality of education and training
therein as one of the best in the Commonwealth.
Needs for services in special education do change. We have made program and service changes
following careful study and planning.
On behalf of parents and children, I would like to thank the school committee members for the
study, discussions and hard decisions that encourage the development of quality in special educa-
tion. Further appreciation goes to the support of administrators, teachers, and non-teaching
staff. Special thanks is extended to the counselors and the nursing staff for their investment in
the special needs of children. Finally, it continues to be a personal and professional pleasure
to work with an outstanding and dedicated special needs faculty. Our efforts are enhanced by the
understanding and assistance of Beth Lawrence, Special Needs Secretary, whose resourcefulness
proves invaluable each year.

Retirements
MARILYN (MAcLEAN) KIBLING (Sept. 1, 1968 - July 20, 1978)
Marilyn, a native of Lunenburg and a graduate of Lunenburg
High School in 1940 and Fitchburg State College in 1944,
receiving her BS degree in Elementary Education, was hired by
the Lunenburg School Committee as a kindergarten teacher for
the 1968-69 school year. This was the first year when kinder-
garten in Lunenburg came under the jurisdiction of the Lunen-
burg Public Schools. Marilyn had had 10½ years of previous
teaching experience in Townsend, Leominster and Fitchburg prior
to her appointment. Over her years of service she took
additional graduate courses, participated in workshops and as
indicated in the elementary principal's report, she played a
major role in the development of Lunenburg's kindergarten curr-
iculum. Mrs. Kibling had requested and received school commit-
tee approval for a year's leave-of-absence for the 1978-79
school year, but on August 10, 1978, she informed school
officials that she had officially retired effective July 20,
1978. All of us wish her many years of retirement and thank
her again for a job well done.
GLORIA (VALITON) MAcPHADDEN (Sept.1, 1957 - June 30,1978)
Gloria is a native daughter of Lunenburg and graduated
from Lunenburg High School in 1933. She received her BSE degree
from Framingham State Teachers' College in June 1937, majoring
in home economics with a minor in clothing. Prior to her
appointment effective September 1957 as a home economics teacher
in the new Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School, she had taught in
Northampton and Fitchburg, MA vocational schools. In acknowl-
edging her intention to retire effective June 30, 1978, the
school committee "wished her the best of future happiness in
her retirement years ahead and thanked her for her conscientious
service to Lunenburg students during her twenty-one years of
service and her efforts and leadership in serving as home
economics department chairperson."
MARY S. THIBAULT (September 1, 1961 February 2, 1979)
Mary was hired as a general worker in the Lunenburg Jr. -Sr. High School cafeteria on Septem-
ber 1, 1961 to replace a worker who had resigned just prior to school opening for the 1961-62
school year. Mary served under four lunch supervisors during her sixteen years of service--Ruth
Correll, Phyllis Prouty, Lucy Chartrand and Marjorie Stillman. Due to grade reorganization effec-
tive with the 1978-79 school year, it was necessary for Mary to be transferred to the Turkey Hill
Middle School cafeteria to serve as cook's helper during preparation time. Unfortunately, due to
illness, Mary has found it necessary to submit her intention to retire effective February 2, 1979.
All of us wish her a speedy recovery and extend our thanks for her contributions to the school
lunch program during her tenure.
- 128 -
===PAGE 131===
GORDON
F. ABARE (August 2, 1971 - February 21, 1979)
Mr. Abare began his employment as a custodian in the Turkey Hill Middle School on August 2,
1971. During his 7½ years of service he was respected by his fellow workers and carried out his
duties in a quiet and orderly manner. Gordon's retirement became effective Wednesday, February 21,
1979 and his fellow workers and other staff members wished him many happy years of retirement at a
farewell party held at the Middle School.
JOHN A. LOVE (March 17, 1969 - September 26, 1970)
(July 12, 1971 - September 1, 1978)
Mr. Love first began as a custodian at the Elementary
School replacing Harold Carrier who had transferred to the
newly opened middle school in 1969. John remained in this
position until September 26, 1970 at which time he resigned to
accept another position. However, when an opening occurred at
the elementary school in 1971, he was rehired and began his
duties on July 12. After serving almost nine years, for personal
reasons, John found it necessary to retire effective September
1, 1978. His pride and care as an elementary custodian was
very much appreciated and will certainly be missed by staff
members. A quiet man, he is respected by all.
ROLAND J. SEGUIN, SR. (December 7, 1970 - July 5, 1978)
On December 7, 1970, Mr. Seguin began his duties as a custodian in the Turkey Hill Middle
School. He remained at the middle school until he applied for and was appointed as Annex custodian
effective November 1, 1973. In February 1978, Roland indicated his intention to retire effective
July 5, 1978, having completed 7½ years of service. He was commended "for his particular pride and
care of the Elementary Annex building which had greatly aided in making the school an attractive
place and pleasant surrounding for both students and teachers.'
In Memory of
ANATOLE LIMOGES (1902 - 1978)
"Nat", as he was known to all of us, passed away at the
Marshall Mall in Leominster where he was employed as a part-time
custodian, on November 21, 1978. He is survived by his wife,
two daughters, six grandchildren and several nieces and nephews.
He and his wife, Evelyn, had celebrated their Golden Wedding
Anniversary on September 17th.
"Nat" first began his service with the Lunenburg Public
Schools as a substitute custodian and on April 6, 1964 he was
appointed as our part-time groundsman. On January 1, 1965, this
position became full-time. His service consisted of duties as
a custodian, groundsman, maintenance man and general handyman.
On May 4, 1972 (his 70th birthday) he retired after eight years
of outstanding, conscientious, cooperative and dependable
service. He was respected by all and will be sorely missed.

#1 - DONALD M. ADAMS, Choral Music Instructor at Lunenburg High School.
A graduate of Mt. Herman School for Boys in 1946, Don received his BM degree in 1950
from Boston University, College of Music and his ME degree in 1963 from Fitchburg State
College. He also has fifteen graduate credits beyond his Master's. Upon discharge
from the United States Navy in 1955, Don joined our staff as Music Supervisor. He also
served as Director of the Jr.-Sr. High School Band and Orchestra. On June 21, 1967, he
resigned to join the staff of Fitchburg State College. However, on September 1, 1970,
he rejoined our music staff as Choral Instructor and Director and has entertained us
with many musical productions over the years. DON COMPLETED 20 YEARS OF SERVICE ON
SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#2 - RICHARD J. ALLAIN, Science teacher at Lunenburg High School.
Dick graduated from Leominster High School in 1950, received his BSE degree from Fitch-
burg SC in 1954 and his ME degree in 1961. He also has 45 graduate credits beyond his
Master's from Framingham SC and Worcester Evening Trade School. Dick also came to us
directly after his discharge from the armed services and was hired in 1956 as a grade
six teacher. In September 1967, he transferred to the senior high school to teach
science including electronics. DICK COMPLETED 22 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#3 - RICHARD J. BISHOP, Grade six teacher at Turkey Hill Middle School.
Dick also graduated from Lunenburg High School in 1952, from Fitchburg SC in 1956 re-
ceiving his BSE degree and in 1962 his M. Ed degree. Thirty graduate credits beyond his
Master's have also been obtained from Fitchburg SC. Dick went on a military leave-of-
absence from September 1, 1957 - September 1, 1959 and upon his return was again
assigned to teach grade six. From 1965 - 1968 he served as teacher-in-charge of grade
six while housed in the junior-senior high school addition. He also served as half-time
administrative assistant to the middle school principal from 1974 - 1977. In 1966,
along with ten other young men, Dick was awarded a Letter of Commendation for being a
recipient of an "Outstanding Young Men Award" by the Greater Worcester Jaycees. He also
has been a very active member of many community and area organizations. DICK COMPLETED
20 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#4 - ANN L. BRAZIL, Grade one teacher at Lunenburg Primary School.
Ann is a graduate of Notre Dame Academy in Tyngsboro, Class of 1948. She received her
BSE degree from Boston University in 1952 and her ME degree from Fitchburg SC in 1957
as well as fifteen graduate credits beyond her Master's. Prior to coming to Lunenburg
in 1954 as a teacher of grade three, Ann had taught two years in Westbury, New York.
ANN COMPLETED 24 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.

#5 - MARILYN J. CORMIER, Grade five teacher at Passios Elementary School.
Marilyn graduated from Leominster High School in 1947 and received her BSE degree from
Fitchburg SC in 1955 and her ME degree in 1966. She also has fifteen graduate credits
beyond her Master's. Marilyn came to Lunenburg following her graduation from college in
1955 being hired as an additional grade four teacher. In 1961 she became a reading
teacher assistant for elementary grades. At the end of the 1961-62 school year, she
resigned to accept a position in East Longmeadow. On September 1, 1963, she returned as
an elementary reading instructor until 1976 when she returned to the classroom as a
seventh grade teacher. MARILYN COMPLETED 22 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.

Hill Middle School Principal and a teacher of grade eight science.
Dick is a native of Fitchburg and graduated from St. Bernards High School in 1947. He
received his BS degree from Fitchburg SC in 1957 and his ME degree in 1961. He has fif-
teen graduate and inservice credits beyond his Master's. Dick came to Lunenburg in 1957
as a teacher of junior high school science and also served as coach of junior high
school boys' basketball for two years and head varsity football coach for twelve years.
He has served as driver education instructor since September 1976. DICK COMPLETED 21
YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#7 - PETER W. GRENIER, Social studies teacher at Lunenburg High School.
Peter graduated from Biddeford, ME High School in 1946, received his BS degree from Gor-
ham STC (Maine) in 1952 and his MA degree from Fitchburg State College in 1958. Before
coming to Lunenburg, he had five years teaching experience in Milo, ME; Dexter, ME; and
Ashby, MA. He was hired in 1957 as a junior high school social studies teacher. From
1957 - 1965 he also coached junior high school boys' basketball and senior high school
junior varsity boys' basketball. PETE COMPLETED 21 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#8 - EILA H. HALLFORS, Reading teacher, grades K through 5.
Graduating from Fitchburg High School in 1938, Eila received her BSE degree from Fitch-
burg SC in 1943 and her ME in 1959. She also has thirty graduate and inservice credits
beyond her Master's. Prior to coming to Lunenburg in 1953 as a teacher of grade two,
she had had 51/2 years of experience in Colrain, MA; Pensacola, FL; Shutesbury, MA; Orange,
MA; Chicago, IL; and Townsend, MA. She served as reading assistant supervisor for
grades K-7 from 1957-1971 when the position was eliminated and her title was changed to
elementary reading specialist. EILA COMPLETES 25 YEARS OF SERVICE IN FEBRUARY 1979.
#9 - JEAN E. KILLEEN, Business Education Department Chairman, Lunenburg High School.
Jean is a graduate of Keith Hall, Lowell, MA in 1953 and received her BSE degree from
Boston College in 1957. She came to Lunenburg in 1957 as a teacher in the business edu-
cation department. She became department head in 1961. During 1959 to 1976, she has
served as cheerleader coach, girls' junior varsity basketball coach, and co-advisor to
the high school creative magazine/newspaper. In the first half of the 1977-78 school
year, Jean took a sabbatical leave to complete her requirements for an ME degree which
she was awarded in June 1978 from Fitchburg State College. JEAN COMPLETED 21 YEARS OF
SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#10 - M. NOREEN KITTREDGE, Grade one teacher at Lunenburg Primary School.
Noreen graduated in 1948 from St. Bernards High School in Fitchburg. She received her
BS degree from Fitchburg SC in 1952 and her ME degree in 1963. She also has thirty
graduate credits beyond her Master's. Noreen had one year of experience in Springfield,
MA when she came to Lunenburg on September 1, 1953 as a grade one teacher. She resigned
in June 1955 and taught the next four years in White Plains, NY and New Canaan, CT., on
January 4, 1960, she returned as a grade three teacher to complete the school year
for a teacher who had resigned. In September 1960 she transferred to grade one.
NOREEN COMPLETED 21 YEARS OF SERVICE IN DECEMBER 1978.

#11 - JOSEPHINE C. MELOON, Foreign Language teacher at Lunenburg High School.
A native of Brownville Jct., Maine, "Jo" graduated from the local high school in 1941.
She received her BA degree from the University of Maine (Orono) in 1947. She has fif-
teen graduate credits beyond the Bachelor's degree from Tufts University. Prior to
coming to Lunenburg in September 1957 as an English teacher, "Jo" had had four years of
prior teaching experience in Temple, Texas; Brownville Jct., Maine; Westminster, Mass.
and Danvers, Mass. During her tenure she has coached junior high school girls' basket-
ball, junior high school girls' softball, and football cheerleaders. "JO" COMPLETED 21
YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
#12 - RICHARD F. MULLIGAN, Physical Education Supervisor and Director of Athletics.
A 1946 graduate of Lunenburg High School, Dick received his BSE degree from Fitchburg
State College in 1950 and his ME in 1955. He also has thirty graduate credits beyond
his Master's. Prior to coming to Lunenburg, Dick had taught one year at the Stevens
School for Boys in Swansea, MA. He was appointed on September 1, 1953 as an additional
teacher in the junior high school due to increased enrollment at that grade level.
Effective September 1, 1957, he transferred to the senior high school level and on
September 1, 1961, he was appointed physical education supervisor and director of ath-
letics, replacing Mr. Floyd Barney. Over the years, Dick has coached junior high boys'
basketball and baseball; intramural programs; senior high boys' junior varsity and
varsity basketball; senior high boys' varsity baseball; junior high-senior high indoor
and outdoor track; and cross country. DICK COMPLETED 25 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER
1, 1978.
#13 - LAWRENCE T. PATON, Senior custodian at Lunenburg High School.
Larry graduated from Lunenburg High School in 1952 and became an assistant custodian in
the new elementary school on August 24, 1953. From January 30, 1957 - February 10, 1959,
Larry left us for service with the United States Marine Corps. When he returned, he was
assigned duties at the junior-senior high school. He became senior custodian in 1965.
LARRY COMPLETED 25 YEARS OF SERVICE ON AUGUST 24, 1978.
#14 - BETHEL E. TAPPLY, Part-time School Psychologist, K-12.
Bethel is a native of Keene, New Hampshire, graduating from its high school in 1935.
She received her BSE degree from Fitchburg State College in 1958 and her ME degree in
1963 from Boston University. She had two years teaching experience in Lebanon, New
Hampshire and Ashburnham, MA, prior to joining the Lunenburg staff as a teacher of
junior high English in September 1958. In 1968, she became school psychologist and a
member of the guidance department, serving as Director from 1969 - 1971. Beginning
with the 1977-78 school year, she requested a less than full-time position and is
serving in a 3/5ths capacity. BETHEL COMPLETED 20 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978.
- 133 -
===PAGE 136===
2
3
The above three individuals represent a total of ninety-eight years having over thirty years
of service with the Lunenburg Public Schools. Can you identify them?
#1 - FLOYD F. BARNEY, Principal of Turkey Hill Middle School.
Floyd is a native of Lunenburg graduating from Fitchburg High School in 1938, receiving
his BS degree from Springfield College in 1947 and his ME degree from Fitchburg State
College in 1955. FLOYD COMPLETED 31 YEARS OF SERVICE ON SEPTEMBER 1, 1978. His service
began in 1947 when he was hired as an additional teacher for the Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High
School. From 1949 - 1961, he coached boys' basketball, boys' baseball, and girls'
softball teams and became Supervisor of Physical Education in 1954-55. On September 1,
1961, he was appointed as Assistant Principal at Lunenburg Jr. -Sr. High School and when
the new middle school was under construction he was appointed as its principal on
July 5, 1967.
#2 - RICHARD J. CAREY, Principal of Lunenburg High School.
Dick is a native of Natick, MA, graduating from its high school in 1938. He received
his AB degree in 1943 from Boston College and his M. Ed from Fitchburg State College in
1952. Prior to coming to Lunenburg in 1946 he had taught a half-year in Natick. ON
SEPTEMBER 1, 1978, DICK COMPLETED 32 YEARS OF SERVICE. He began his service with the
Lunenburg Public Schools as a teacher and during his first three years also coached
high school boys' basketball. On November 1, 1948, he was appointed as Principal of
Lunenburg Junior High School until September 1, 1958 when he became Assistant Principal
of Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School. On August 14, 1961 he was appointed as its Principal.
#3 - VIRGINIA F. WELCH, Executive Secretary to the Superintendent of Schools and Clerk to the
School Committee, COMPLETING 35 YEARS OF SERVICE ON DECEMBER 26, 1978.
"Ginny," as she is known systemwide, is a native daughter of Lunenburg, graduating from
Lunenburg High School with the Class of 1943. When she began her service back in 1943,
Lunenburg was a member of Union #24 consisting of Ashby-Lunenburg-Townsend, with the
superintendent's office located in the Spaulding Memorial School in Townsend. In 1950,
more office space was needed, and the office was relocated in its present quarters
(Brooks House) in Lunenburg. On July 1, 1954, Lunenburg withdrew from the union and
continued as a single school system. In her 35 years, Ginny has worked for five super-
intendents--Charles M. Robinson (retired, living in New Hampshire) from 1943-1949;
Maurice P. Billings (retired, living in Townsend, MA) from 1949-1962; Ralph W. Brown
(retired, living in Florida) from 1962-1970; Dr. William R. Holland (Superintendent of
Schools in Narragansett, Rhode Island) from 1970-1978; and presently, Dr. William C.
Allard since August 1, 1978. With only five superintendents in a thirty-five year
period it certainly speaks well for the high quality school system that Lunenburg has.
Ginny has experienced all phases of the central office operation as receptionist, payroll
clerk, bookkeeper, consultant, and executive secretary.

School Nursing Report
by JEAN E. HOBBS, R. N.
Coordinating Nurse
This year, a concentrated immuniza-
tion program was conducted by the school
nursing staff. A complete check was made
of all student health records to identify
students in need of immunization, using
the criteria set by the Massachusetts
Department of Public Health. Considerable
time was spent in interpreting immuniza-
tion needs to parents, initiating contacts
to private physicians, and organizing
clinics, held with the assistance of the
nursing staff from Nashoba Associated
Boards of Health. Total immunizations ad-
ministered were: 12 DPT; 236 Td;48 Polio;
101 Measles; and 137 MMR. Many parents
Mrs. Hobbs tests for student hearing.
elected the option of having students
immunized by their own physician.
The end result was that all Lunenburg students were certified as having complete immunizations,
and the administration was not forced to exclude any student, as would have been required had not
the program been successful. Credit is due, not only to the nursing staff, but to classroom
teachers and administrators who cooperated in the efforts to contact students and in the conduct-
ing of the clinics.
Other health programs carried out in conjunction with Nashoba Associated Boards of Health are
the tuberculosis testing and dental programs. Mantoux tests for tuberculosis were done on 84 of
the school personnel, and tine tests were done on 92 children in first grade. Dental Hygienists
from Nashoba offer fluoride treatments to students in grades two, five, and eight, and students in
kindergarten and grade three may have their teeth checked.
In addition to required vision and
hearing screening, postural screening is
done on students in grades five through
eight. Participating students are screened
initially in gym class by the physical
education instructor, and re-screening and
necessary referral is completed by the
school nurse. A blood pressure screening
for students in grade eight is being in-
stituted this year.
Immunization monitoring and screen-
ing programs are an important part of a
school health program, but they are by no
means all the functions of the nursing
staff. School health services include
early identification of pupil health prob-
lems, pupil health counseling, counseling
parents in regard to health care re-
sources, and then motivating parents to
obtain whatever health care is needed for
their children, providing adequate care
Mrs. Richards tests for blood pressure.
for health emergencies, either injuries or
illness, and maintaining and interpreting
accurate pupil health records. Health counseling is also provided for school personnel as well
as annual blood pressure screening, periodic monitoring of blood pressure, and yearly flu shots.
- 165
===PAGE 168===
In the case of a very young child, it is more often the parent who benefits from a conference
or counseling session with the school nurse. Middle school and particularly the high school stu-
dents seek out the nurse with questions and problems. The nurses on all levels work closely with
the guidance department to help maintain optimum health for students. The school nurses are
available as health resource people to those conducting the health education phase of the school
health program. With the opening of a health information center adjacent to the high school health
office, there is an excellent opportunity for the nurse to assist students with inquiries.
Increasingly, parents are requesting that school nurses check students to determine the need
for medical care, for both illness and home injury. The diagnostic skills of the nurses are appre-
ciated in this era of working parents with limited time, and the high cost of medical care. Once
the assessment is made, careful follow-ups are necessary to avoid misjudgment.
The school nursing staff is composed of Jean E. Hobbs, R. N., full-time coordinating nurse
and nurse for the T. C. Passios and Primary Schools; Marie B. ("Ginger") Richards, R. N., part-
time nurse at the Turkey Hill Middle School; and Jean M. Larkin, R. N., part-time nurse at the
High School. Two physicians share the position of school physician. Dr. Joel Neuschatz is pri-
marily responsible for sports physicals, and Dr. Gary Asher provides coverage for physicals for
younger students, consultation for the school health program, and is a member of the Health Educa-
tion Curriculum Committee, as are Mrs. Hobbs and Mrs. Larkin.
Not only do nurses serve
as health resource people,
but they impart much infor-
mal health education infor-
mation to students and par-
ticipate in a variety of
ways in formal health edu-
cation. Mrs. Larkin, a cer-
tified PR(Cardio-Pulmonary
Resusitation) instructor,
together with the physical
education staff, has started
a CPR course for senior
class members. This program
hopefully will be expanded,
with the goal to have all
students graduating from
Lunenburg High School cer-
tified in CPR. Also, mini-
programs, using area health
resource people, are being
presented at the high school
to cover such topics as acne
and nutrition, as well as
more wide-range program on
breast self-examination.M
Richards is assisting in
first aid classes and other
health education segments
at the Middle School. Nurse
visits to the classroom to
cover a variety of health
related issues will be con-
tinued by Mrs. Hobbs on the
elementary and primary level.
Lunenburg school nurses
make home visits to gather
birth and developmentalhis-
C. P. R.
tory for students under-
(Cardio-Pulmonary Resuscitation)
going core evaluation serve
(L-R): Lorraine White, George Starr,
as a liason with private
Jean Larkin, R. N., School Nurse
physicians, and as medical
resource person at core
- 166
===PAGE 169===
evaluation team meetings as part of Chapter 766 proceedings. We are now dealing with students
having extensive physical handicaps and chronic illnesses, SO the nurse must be prepared to assist
in developing a care plan, as well as an educational plan for these students.
The school nurses participate in in-service programs available through area health care
facilities, and are members of the American School Health Association and the Massachusetts School
Nurses' Association. Bi-monthly sessions are held with Dr. Asher to keep the school health pro-
gram current, and to discuss the various medical conditions and problems encountered in the
schools.
The focus of the school health program is on preventative health measures and early detection
of possible health problems. To effectively carry out this program, Lunenburg school nurses are
functioning as evaluators, health counselors, interpreters, consultants and as an integral part of
the Lunenburg educational system.
School Lunch Program Report
by MARJORIE H. STILLMAN, School Lunch Supervisor
Our present high school student body does not know what it is
like to have only a Type "A" lunch or what I refer to as a "tray
lunch." In 1974 I began a simple a la carte service. Like
"Topsy" it grew. With it came problems. A cafeteria designed to
prepare and serve one-type meal must change in many ways to
accommodate an a la carte service--a service offering many types
of foods. I began by making the center of our line into this
service. The cash registers went outside the kitchen on mobile
units and a display and service case installed in their place.
A la carte really "took off." For over a year there was crowding,
pushing, pilfering. Something had to be done. I certainly wouldn't have battled the crowd as the
student had to participate. Mr. Carey permitted me to try a new scheme. One end of our line was
designated "tray meals," the other "a la carte." This eliminated the center scramble, but on the
days we served a very popular meal, the tray line was long and slow.
In the spring of 1978 I began planning to establish our present a la carte service. The
promise of delivery for September opening never materialized. At last on January 2, we opened our
new a la carte line which consists of three mobile units and a milk cooler. A previously purchased
refrigerator for a la carte was moved from the kitchen. We now have a separate unit operating
under, but outside our present set up. Sue Cioffi has been appointed to oversee its operation
working with Mrs. Beaulac, Cafeteria Manager. We are in full operation offering soup, salads,
sandwiches, a hot entree, fresh fruit, milk shakes, hot chocolate, ice cream and yogurt. It is
working beautifully.
- 167 -
===PAGE 170===
It's new! A-la-carte!
Of interest to the taxpayer, I would like to make note that this equipment purchased for my
program comes from my revolving account and not from your school budget. The revolving account is
monies from sales and state and federal reimbursements based on meal and milk counts. I have been
able in many cases to get help from non-foods assistance,a federal program paying 75% and I paying
25% from my revolving account.
AT THE MIDDLE SCHOOL: "A la carte is great!" The word got to the Middle School. In April,
Mr. Barney asked me if I could do something in a la carte for his school. We ran a trial program
for the month of May. It was a huge success-the children were excited; the faculty supportive.
We offer a simple service here--soup, salads, sandwiches, milk, and a sweet.
I'm really proud of my program. I have a wonderful group of women carrying out my program.
The pupils seem happy, the faculty complimentary. How great it makes me feel when a faculty member
comes back from visiting a school somewhere and commends our program--that is when I grow a little
taller.
